Production Cloud Infrastructure & AI/MLOps Engineering — Combined Volumes 1–2

Hinglish production engineering textbook | October 2026

Combined contents

  • Volume 1: Chapters 1–25, AWS/Linux/Kubernetes/Jenkins/Bedrock/SageMaker foundations and first deployment.
  • Volume 2: Chapters 26–35, Terraform, Ansible, Helm, Apache, Nginx, NVIDIA GPU, MLflow, Kubeflow and integrated production operations.

Production Cloud Infrastructure & AI/MLOps Engineering

Volume 1 — Foundations, Reference Architecture and First Production Deployment

Language: Hinglish with standard English AWS and Kubernetes terminology. Audience: infrastructure engineers moving into production AWS/DevOps/MLOps.

Reader agreement

This is Volume 1 of a larger master book. It contains a complete curriculum, deeply worked foundational chapters, and an executable reference deployment. Remaining subjects are introduced in the curriculum and expanded in subsequent volumes; the curriculum list alone is not full chapter coverage. Commands are educational examples: validate region, pricing, permissions, service quotas and organization policy before using them.

Part I — Production thinking and architecture

Chapter 1 — What production-ready actually means

1.1 Concept and why

Local laptop par application chalna deployment ka proof hai, production readiness ka nahi. Production means users can reach the service securely, changes are reproducible, failures are observable, and recovery is practiced. A good design defines measurable SLOs, an explicit threat model, ownership, on-call runbooks, backups and cost limits.

1.2 Control plane versus data plane

  • Control plane: APIs and controllers that declare, validate and reconcile desired state. Example: EKS API server and controllers, ECS service scheduler, Terraform planning.
  • Data plane: resources handling real requests, e.g. ALB, containers, network paths, model inference.
  • Reconciliation: controllers compare actual state with desired state and repeatedly act to close the gap; this is not a single imperative script.

1.3 Request workflow

User -> Route 53 DNS -> HTTPS ALB -> ECS Fargate FastAPI
                                      |
                                      +-> Bedrock Runtime -> managed FM
                                      +-> SageMaker endpoint -> custom model
                                      +-> S3 -> versioned objects
Observability: CloudWatch logs, metrics, alarms; audit: CloudTrail
Deployment: Git -> Jenkins -> tests -> ECR -> ECS deployment

DNS lookup is separate from the HTTP request. Route 53 returns records; the client connects to the ALB. TLS terminates at the HTTPS listener unless you explicitly design re-encryption. ECS tasks should be in private subnets; ALB typically in public subnets. Never assume a private subnet has internet access without NAT or required VPC endpoints.

1.4 Production acceptance checklist

  • Two Availability Zones where the service and chosen dependencies support it.
  • Private application tasks; public ingress only through approved load balancer.
  • Least-privilege task role and separate execution role.
  • Health endpoints distinguish liveness from readiness.
  • Structured logs, alarms, actionable runbooks, and synthetic checks.
  • Secrets in managed secret stores, not Docker images or Git.
  • Reproducible infrastructure and rollback strategy.
  • Budget alarms and resource cleanup plan.

1.5 Interview scenario

Q: Why does an ALB health check pass but users receive 500?

A: A successful /health may only prove the web server is listening. Investigate target-group metrics, request traces, application exceptions, downstream Bedrock/SageMaker permissions, throttling, connection pools and dependency timeouts. Readiness should cover critical dependencies carefully without creating cascading failures.

Part II — AWS networking and identity

Chapter 2 — VPC design from first principles

2.1 CIDR and subnetting

A VPC 10.20.0.0/16 has 65,536 IPv4 addresses in its range. Divide into subnet CIDRs per AZ; for example 10.20.0.0/24 public-a, 10.20.1.0/24 public-b, 10.20.10.0/24 private-a, 10.20.11.0/24 private-b. AWS reserves five IPv4 addresses in each subnet. A /24 therefore has 251 usable private addresses in AWS. Avoid overlapping CIDRs with on-prem and peered networks.

2.2 Route decision and security evaluation

Routing uses longest-prefix match among eligible routes. A public subnet generally has a default route 0.0.0.0/0 -> internet gateway and workloads need public addresses for direct IPv4 internet communication. A private subnet may route outbound IPv4 via a NAT gateway in a public subnet, or use endpoints for supported AWS services. NAT gateway does not permit unsolicited inbound internet connections.

Security groups are stateful and attached to ENIs; network ACLs are stateless and operate at subnet boundaries. SG rules allow traffic; NACLs support allow and deny rules. Return traffic is automatically tracked by SGs, but NACLs require explicit return-path rules.

2.3 Packet walkthrough

Internet client -> DNS ALB name -> public ALB ENI (443)
-> ALB listener/rule -> target group -> private ECS task ENI (8080)
-> task IAM role signs AWS API request -> Bedrock Runtime endpoint
-> response -> ALB -> client

ALB security group allows inbound 443 from approved clients. Task SG allows inbound 8080 from ALB SG. Task outbound must reach AWS APIs via appropriate endpoint or NAT. ALB target health checks use the target group health path and port.

2.4 Troubleshooting runbook

  1. aws ec2 describe-route-tables --filters Name=vpc-id,Values=$VPC_ID — inspect selected subnet associations and default routes.
  2. aws ec2 describe-security-groups --group-ids $ALB_SG $TASK_SG — verify ingress/egress, source SG references.
  3. aws elbv2 describe-target-health --target-group-arn "$TG_ARN" — inspect reason codes.
  4. Enable VPC Flow Logs for rejected/accepted traffic; remember they do not explain application-layer 500 errors.
  5. Check DNS resolution and TLS with dig +short app.example.com and curl -v https://app.example.com/health.

2.5 Interview questions

Q: Does an internet gateway make every subnet public? No. Route table, addressing and SG/NACL configuration all matter.

Q: Why use gateway endpoints for S3? Private VPC access to S3 without NAT for supported routes, reducing exposure and often data-processing costs; verify regional and routing limitations.

Chapter 3 — IAM, STS and least privilege

3.1 Principal, policy, resource, action, condition

IAM evaluates applicable identity policies, resource policies, permission boundaries, SCPs and session policies. Explicit deny overrides allow. A role has a trust policy determining who can assume it and a permissions policy describing what it may do after assumption. STS issues temporary credentials. Avoid distributing long-lived access keys to applications.

3.2 ECS task role vs task execution role

  • Task execution role: ECS agent uses it to pull from ECR and send logs (and retrieve referenced secrets when configured).
  • Task role: application code inside container uses it to invoke Bedrock, S3 or SageMaker.
  • Do not attach broad AdministratorAccess to either role. Scope model IDs/ARNS and actions to the actual application.

3.3 Example task-role policy

{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [
    {
      "Sid": "InvokeApprovedBedrockModel",
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": ["bedrock:InvokeModel"],
      "Resource": ["arn:aws:bedrock:us-east-1::foundation-model/amazon.titan-text-express-v1"]
    }
  ]
}

This example is intentionally narrow and model-specific; choose an available model and update the ARN, action set and region to match your application. Some Bedrock invocation modes, inference profiles and APIs require additional resource ARNs or permissions. Verify against the current service authorization reference.

3.4 Test permissions

aws sts get-caller-identity
aws iam simulate-principal-policy \
  --policy-source-arn "$TASK_ROLE_ARN" \
  --action-names bedrock:InvokeModel \
  --resource-arns "$MODEL_ARN"

The simulator is a diagnostic aid, not a complete substitute for an end-to-end request: resource policies, SCPs, conditions and context may affect real authorization.

3.5 Failure scenario

Symptom: container logs show AccessDeniedException. Investigate: task role ARN, deployed task definition revision, resource ARN, model entitlement, IAM policy conditions, organization SCPs and endpoint policy. Do not solve by assigning administrator privileges.

Part III — First working application

Chapter 4 — Bedrock + FastAPI + Boto3

4.1 Architecture and prerequisites

Use Python 3.11+, FastAPI, boto3 and a Bedrock-supported model enabled for the target account/region. This sample uses the Bedrock converse API and a configurable model identifier. Account/model access and pricing vary. The ECS task role needs bedrock:InvokeModel on relevant resources.

4.2 Application code (app.py)

import os
import logging
import boto3
from botocore.config import Config
from fastapi import FastAPI, HTTPException
from pydantic import BaseModel, Field

log = logging.getLogger("inference")
logging.basicConfig(level=logging.INFO)
app = FastAPI()
region = os.environ.get("AWS_REGION", "us-east-1")
model_id = os.environ["BEDROCK_MODEL_ID"]
client = boto3.client(
    "bedrock-runtime",
    region_name=region,
    config=Config(connect_timeout=5, read_timeout=60, retries={"mode": "standard", "max_attempts": 3}),
)

class Prompt(BaseModel):
    text: str = Field(min_length=1, max_length=2000)

@app.get("/health")
def health():
    return {"status": "ok"}

@app.post("/generate")
def generate(payload: Prompt):
    try:
        response = client.converse(
            modelId=model_id,
            messages=[{"role": "user", "content": [{"text": payload.text}]}],
            inferenceConfig={"maxTokens": 300, "temperature": 0.2},
        )
        parts = response["output"]["message"]["content"]
        return {"answer": "".join(p.get("text", "") for p in parts)}
    except Exception:
        log.exception("Bedrock invocation failed")
        raise HTTPException(status_code=502, detail="Model invocation failed")

Line-by-line explanation: boto3.client uses the standard AWS credential provider chain (on ECS, task credentials); Config sets network timeouts and retry strategy; Field limits payload size; /health is a lightweight process check; converse sends a typed message and caps output tokens; exceptions are logged server-side without leaking internal AWS details to clients. Production upgrades: authentication, rate limiting, request IDs, input moderation, output guardrails, streaming, cost accounting and tracing.

4.3 Dependencies (requirements.txt)

fastapi>=0.115,<1
uvicorn>=0.30,<1
boto3>=1.35,<2

4.4 Container image (Dockerfile)

FROM python:3.12-slim
WORKDIR /srv
RUN useradd --create-home --uid 10001 appuser
COPY requirements.txt ./
RUN pip install --no-cache-dir -r requirements.txt
COPY app.py ./
USER appuser
EXPOSE 8080
CMD ["uvicorn", "app:app", "--host", "0.0.0.0", "--port", "8080"]

Use pinned image digests and locked dependency versions in a production pipeline; scan both the base image and Python dependencies. Non-root is necessary but not sufficient: apply read-only root filesystem where compatible and drop unnecessary capabilities.

4.5 Local lab

python3 -m venv .venv && source .venv/bin/activate
pip install -r requirements.txt
export AWS_REGION=us-east-1
export BEDROCK_MODEL_ID="REPLACE_WITH_SUPPORTED_MODEL_ID"
uvicorn app:app --host 127.0.0.1 --port 8080
curl -s http://127.0.0.1:8080/health
curl -s -X POST http://127.0.0.1:8080/generate \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"text":"Explain AWS IAM in two sentences"}'

Expected /health: {"status":"ok"}. /generate returns an answer on success. A 502 means inspect application logs, AWS region, model ID, IAM, account model availability and network path.

4.6 Interview Q&A

Q: Why does Boto3 not need keys in the container? The AWS SDK credential chain obtains temporary task-role credentials from the ECS-provided credentials endpoint.

Q: How do you prevent runaway inference spend? Authentication, request quotas, input/output token limits, budgets, CloudWatch metrics and application-level per-tenant usage accounting.

Chapter 5 — ECS Fargate production deployment workflow

5.1 Prerequisites

You need an AWS account, configured AWS CLI, two-AZ VPC, private subnets, public ALB subnets, NAT or required VPC endpoints, ECR repository, ACM certificate, Route 53 hosted zone, security groups, CloudWatch log group, ECS cluster and IAM roles. Terraform should create these resources; do not hard-code secrets or account IDs in source.

5.2 Build and push image

export AWS_REGION=us-east-1
export ACCOUNT_ID=$(aws sts get-caller-identity --query Account --output text)
export REPO=production-ai-api
aws ecr create-repository --repository-name "$REPO" --region "$AWS_REGION"
aws ecr get-login-password --region "$AWS_REGION" | \
  docker login --username AWS --password-stdin "$ACCOUNT_ID.dkr.ecr.$AWS_REGION.amazonaws.com"
docker build -t "$REPO:demo" .
docker tag "$REPO:demo" "$ACCOUNT_ID.dkr.ecr.$AWS_REGION.amazonaws.com/$REPO:demo"
docker push "$ACCOUNT_ID.dkr.ecr.$AWS_REGION.amazonaws.com/$REPO:demo"

For production, tag images with the immutable Git commit SHA and deploy by image digest, not a mutable latest tag.

5.3 ECS task definition template (task-definition.json)

{
  "family": "production-ai-api",
  "networkMode": "awsvpc",
  "requiresCompatibilities": ["FARGATE"],
  "cpu": "512",
  "memory": "1024",
  "executionRoleArn": "REPLACE_EXECUTION_ROLE_ARN",
  "taskRoleArn": "REPLACE_TASK_ROLE_ARN",
  "containerDefinitions": [{
    "name": "api",
    "image": "REPLACE_IMAGE_DIGEST_URI",
    "essential": true,
    "portMappings": [{"containerPort": 8080, "protocol": "tcp"}],
    "environment": [{"name": "BEDROCK_MODEL_ID", "value": "REPLACE_MODEL_ID"}],
    "logConfiguration": {
      "logDriver": "awslogs",
      "options": {
        "awslogs-group": "/ecs/production-ai-api",
        "awslogs-region": "us-east-1",
        "awslogs-stream-prefix": "ecs"
      }
    }
  }]
}

Task sizing must match supported Fargate CPU/memory combinations. Replace placeholders before registration. The log group should exist with retention and encryption policy configured.

5.4 Deployment sequence

  1. Create ECS cluster and register the task definition.
  2. Create an IP-type target group on port 8080 with /health health checks.
  3. Configure HTTPS ALB listener with ACM certificate; redirect HTTP to HTTPS if HTTP listener exists.
  4. Create ECS service using Fargate launch type or a capacity provider strategy, private subnets and task SG.
  5. Set desired count to at least 2 and distribute across AZs; confirm load-balancer registration and health.
  6. Set service autoscaling and deployment circuit breaker with rollback, based on workload and deployment configuration.
  7. Create Route 53 alias record pointing to ALB; verify DNS, certificate and application behavior.

5.5 Operational commands

aws ecs describe-services --cluster "$CLUSTER" --services "$SERVICE"
aws ecs list-tasks --cluster "$CLUSTER" --service-name "$SERVICE"
aws ecs describe-tasks --cluster "$CLUSTER" --tasks $TASK_ARNS
aws logs tail /ecs/production-ai-api --since 30m --follow
aws elbv2 describe-target-health --target-group-arn "$TG_ARN"

Do not paste untrusted task IDs into shell scripts; use arrays or validated output for robust automation.

5.6 Troubleshooting matrix

Symptom Likely causes First checks
Task stuck in PENDING capacity, image pull, networking ECS stopped reason, task events, ECR connectivity
ALB target unhealthy SG, wrong port/path, slow boot target health reason, app logs, health response
502 from ALB target closes connection, protocol mismatch ALB metrics, target logs, listener/target settings
Bedrock AccessDenied task role, model resource, SCP task role, CloudTrail, region and policy
Timeout to AWS API no NAT/endpoint, DNS, endpoint SG VPC routes, endpoints, DNS, flow logs
Latency spikes throttling, model time, retries p95/p99, downstream errors, token usage

Chapter 6 — Monitoring, SLOs and incident response

6.1 Four golden signals

Latency (p50/p95/p99), traffic (RPS), errors (5xx and failed invocations), saturation (CPU, memory, concurrency, throttling). Monitor user-facing behavior rather than CPU alone. ECS CPU utilization does not explain all Bedrock latency.

6.2 Example SLO

Availability SLO: 99.9% successful requests over 30 days, excluding explicitly documented client errors. Error budget is approximately 43.2 minutes in a 30-day month. Define precise numerator/denominator and distinguish application 5xx from downstream provider throttling.

6.3 CloudWatch alarm example

aws cloudwatch put-metric-alarm \
  --alarm-name production-ai-alb-5xx \
  --namespace AWS/ApplicationELB \
  --metric-name HTTPCode_Target_5XX_Count \
  --statistic Sum --period 60 --evaluation-periods 3 \
  --threshold 10 --comparison-operator GreaterThanThreshold \
  --dimensions Name=LoadBalancer,Value="$ALB_DIMENSION" \
  --treat-missing-data notBreaching

This is a simple demonstration alarm, not a universal threshold. In production prefer error-rate alarms, traffic-aware burn-rate alerts and an explicit notification action. The load-balancer dimension is the ARN suffix, not the full ARN.

6.4 Incident workflow

Alert -> acknowledge -> assess customer impact -> mitigate
      -> capture evidence -> root cause -> prevention action

Use deployment rollback first when a release correlates with incident onset. Preserve logs and timestamps. After recovery, write a blameless postmortem with impact, timeline, contributing factors and tracked fixes.

Chapter 7 — Kubernetes and EKS preview: production manifests

The same FastAPI app can run on EKS; Bedrock inference is still an AWS API call, not a local model. Use EKS Pod Identity or IRSA for scoped credentials; the pod service account must be correctly bound to its role.

apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: ai-api
spec:
  replicas: 2
  selector:
    matchLabels: {app: ai-api}
  template:
    metadata:
      labels: {app: ai-api}
    spec:
      serviceAccountName: ai-api
      containers:
        - name: api
          image: REPLACE_IMAGE_DIGEST_URI
          ports: [{containerPort: 8080}]
          env:
            - name: BEDROCK_MODEL_ID
              value: REPLACE_MODEL_ID
          resources:
            requests: {cpu: "250m", memory: "256Mi"}
            limits: {memory: "512Mi"}
          readinessProbe:
            httpGet: {path: /health, port: 8080}
            initialDelaySeconds: 5
            periodSeconds: 10
          livenessProbe:
            httpGet: {path: /health, port: 8080}
            initialDelaySeconds: 15
            periodSeconds: 20
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: Service
metadata:
  name: ai-api
spec:
  selector: {app: ai-api}
  ports: [{port: 80, targetPort: 8080}]
  type: ClusterIP

Deployment creates ReplicaSets and pods; Service gives stable virtual addressing; readiness determines which pods receive Service traffic. Liveness restarts stuck containers. This manifest intentionally does not include an Ingress/Gateway, ServiceAccount resource, PodDisruptionBudget, autoscaling, network policy, topology spread or TLS configuration; these must be added before production.

Debug commands

kubectl get deploy,rs,pod,svc -l app=ai-api -o wide
kubectl describe pod -l app=ai-api
kubectl logs deploy/ai-api --tail=100
kubectl rollout status deployment/ai-api
kubectl rollout undo deployment/ai-api
kubectl get events --sort-by=.lastTimestamp | tail -30

Interview: What if pod Ready but ALB says unhealthy? Inspect ingress controller configuration, target-type (instance/IP), security groups, listener rules, health path and network policies. Kubernetes readiness and ALB health checks are independent mechanisms.

Chapter 8 — SageMaker deployment mental model

SageMaker real-time inference generally separates Model (container and artifacts), EndpointConfig (production variants and instance configuration), and Endpoint (deployed serving address). The container must satisfy SageMaker serving contract including health/ping and inference handling for the chosen deployment pattern. Autoscaling, model monitor, network isolation and rollout strategy are distinct configuration concerns.

S3 model.tar.gz + ECR inference image
               |
               v
        SageMaker Model
               |
               v
        EndpointConfig
               |
               v
        Endpoint (HTTPS API)
               |
               v
       boto3 invoke_endpoint

Invocation example

import json
import boto3
runtime = boto3.client("sagemaker-runtime", region_name="us-east-1")
response = runtime.invoke_endpoint(
    EndpointName="REPLACE_ENDPOINT_NAME",
    ContentType="application/json",
    Body=json.dumps({"instances": [[1.0, 2.0, 3.0]]}),
)
print(response["Body"].read().decode("utf-8"))

Payload shape is model-specific: the sample only works when the deployed inference container expects instances. SageMaker endpoints incur hosting costs while running.

Failure analysis

Endpoint creation failure: inspect describe-endpoint, production variant status, image availability, container startup logs, model artifact access, role permissions, memory sizing and health-check timeout. A 4xx from invocation may be a malformed model payload; 5xx may indicate model-container exceptions or service faults.

Chapter 9 — Jenkins delivery pipeline example

pipeline {
  agent any
  options { timestamps(); disableConcurrentBuilds() }
  environment {
    AWS_REGION = "us-east-1"
    ECR_REPO = "production-ai-api"
  }
  stages {
    stage("Test") {
      steps { sh "python3 -m compileall -q app.py" }
    }
    stage("Build") {
      steps { sh "docker build -t ${ECR_REPO}:${GIT_COMMIT} ." }
    }
    stage("Security gate") {
      steps { echo "Add mandatory image/SBOM/secret scanning and policy checks here" }
    }
    stage("Deploy") {
      steps { echo "Promote immutable digest, update ECS task definition, wait for stability, smoke-test" }
    }
  }
}

This is a teaching skeleton, NOT a complete production Jenkinsfile. A real implementation must configure isolated agents, ephemeral AWS identity (OIDC/role assumption where supported), test gates, ECR push, digest capture, signed artifact provenance, ECS service update, rollback, protected approvals and notification. Avoid granting Jenkins a standing administrator key.

Chapter 10 — Production exercises and interview evaluation

Lab A — Diagnose a private-subnet timeout

Given: ALB health green, /generate returns 502, CloudWatch logs show SDK connection timeout. Determine whether the Bedrock endpoint is reachable via NAT or supported interface VPC endpoint; check endpoint private DNS, task SG egress, endpoint SG ingress on 443, route tables and VPC DNS settings. Confirm IAM separately because network errors and AccessDenied require different fixes.

Lab B — Restore after failed deployment

Inject a wrong container port in a staging task definition. Observe ECS events, ALB target health and deployment failure. Revert to the last healthy task definition; record detection time, rollback time and blast radius. Never inject failures into production without a controlled change window.

Lab C — Interview (senior infrastructure)

Q: ECS or EKS? ECS is operationally simpler for AWS-native container orchestration; EKS is appropriate where Kubernetes ecosystem, portability, custom controllers or platform standardization justify its complexity. Both can run on Fargate with different limitations.

Q: Bedrock or SageMaker? Bedrock offers managed foundation-model access and GenAI capabilities; SageMaker AI provides broader custom model development and hosting workflows. Choose based on model control, serving needs, cost, compliance and team skills.

Q: What is your rollback strategy? Immutable artifact, previous task definition or deployment revision, health-gated release, database backward compatibility, tested rollback and clear stop criteria.

Volume 4: Jenkins, Boto3, Lambda, event systems, observability, CloudWatch, incident response and security pipelines.

Chapter 26 — Production reference platform

26.1 What are we building?

Ek enterprise AI service imagine karo: users HTTPS se FastAPI inference gateway ko call karte hain. Gateway policy ke basis par Bedrock managed model ya self-hosted vLLM endpoint use karta hai. Training workflow Kubeflow Pipelines orchestrate karta hai; MLflow experiment metrics aur model versions track karta hai. Terraform AWS networking/EKS/S3/IAM banata hai; Ansible dedicated Linux hosts configure karta hai; Helm EKS workloads deploy karta hai; Jenkins CI/CD automate karta hai. CloudWatch aur Prometheus/Grafana telemetry collect karte hain.

Users -> Route 53 -> ALB (TLS) -> EKS Ingress -> FastAPI gateway
                                                 |-> Bedrock Runtime (IAM + VPC endpoint where supported)
                                                 `-> vLLM Service -> GPU Pods -> model weights
Developer -> Git -> Jenkins -> tests/scan -> ECR -> Helm -> EKS
                         |-> Terraform plan (approved apply)
ML data -> S3 -> Kubeflow Pipeline -> training job -> MLflow -> model registry
                                                       `-> approved deployment
Metrics: CloudWatch / Prometheus / DCGM Exporter -> alerts -> on-call

Nonfunctional requirements: 99.9% monthly service SLO as a sample business target, p95 latency measured against workload-specific target, multi-AZ nodes, least-privilege workload identities, encryption at rest/in transit, audit trail, versioned model artifacts, immutable image digests, tested restore and rollback. An SLO is not automatically achieved by selecting managed services: load tests and failure tests must validate it.

Control plane vs data plane: Terraform, Kubernetes API, Jenkins, Kubeflow control/configuration decisions lete hain. User requests, network packets, GPU inference aur model artifacts data plane mein flow karte hain. Control-plane failure se existing requests kabhi-kabhi chal sakti hain, par scaling/deployment/recovery block ho sakti hai.

26.2 Repository structure

platform/
  terraform/environments/{dev,stage,prod}/
  terraform/modules/{network,eks,storage,identity}/
  ansible/{inventories,group_vars,roles,playbooks}/
  charts/inference-gateway/{Chart.yaml,values.yaml,templates/}
  apps/gateway/{app.py,requirements.txt,Dockerfile}/
  ml/{train.py,pipeline.py}/
  ci/Jenkinsfile
  runbooks/{alb-5xx.md,gpu-unavailable.md,mlflow-outage.md}/

Change gates: pull request → static validation → tests → security scans → plan/diff review → staging deploy → smoke/load tests → production approval → canary → observe → full rollout or rollback. Prod apply ke liye human approval aur auditable role use karo.

Chapter 27 — Terraform production engineering

27.1 Internals: why state exists

Terraform declarative desired state (.tf) aur provider API ke actual resources ko reconcile karta hai. terraform plan config, state aur refresh data compare karke action graph banata hai. State remote objects ke IDs aur attributes map karta hai; yeh password nahi hai lekin sensitive information contain kar sakta hai. Isliye encrypted remote backend, restricted IAM, versioning, access logging aur locking critical hain. State manually edit mat karo jab tak documented recovery procedure na ho.

Important distinction: depends_on explicit ordering deta hai, but normal resource references se implicit dependency derive hoti hai. Terraform resource creation ke baad bhi application readiness guarantee nahi karta. Provider schema, eventual consistency aur service quotas deployment failures cause kar sakte hain.

27.2 Minimal AWS environment with remote state

Bootstrap S3 state bucket separately create karo (existing state ko usi backend se bootstrap nahi karna chahiye). Bucket versioning, SSE-KMS, Block Public Access, restrictive bucket policy and access logging configure karo. Modern Terraform S3 backend use_lockfile = true support karta hai; older configurations may use DynamoDB locking, which is deprecated in current Terraform releases. Validate installed Terraform version before migration.

File: terraform/environments/dev/backend.tf

terraform {
  required_version = ">= 1.10.0"
  required_providers {
    aws = { source = "hashicorp/aws", version = "~> 6.0" }
  }
  backend "s3" {
    bucket       = "REPLACE-unique-company-tfstate"
    key          = "ai-platform/dev/terraform.tfstate"
    region       = "ap-south-1"
    encrypt      = true
    use_lockfile = true
  }
}
provider "aws" {
  region = var.aws_region
  default_tags { tags = { Project = "ai-platform", Environment = var.environment, ManagedBy = "terraform" } }
}
variable "aws_region" { type = string; default = "ap-south-1" }
variable "environment" { type = string; default = "dev" }

required_version: compatible Terraform CLI enforce karta hai. required_providers: provider source/version range constrain karta hai; .terraform.lock.hcl commit karo to resolved versions pin hon. backend: remote state location define karta hai; backend config mein credentials mat rakho. use_lockfile: concurrent state changes ko serialize karne mein help karta hai. default_tags: common cost ownership metadata.

File: terraform/environments/dev/main.tf (educational network slice)

resource "aws_vpc" "main" {
  cidr_block           = "10.40.0.0/16"
  enable_dns_support   = true
  enable_dns_hostnames = true
  tags = { Name = "ai-dev-vpc" }
}
resource "aws_subnet" "private_a" {
  vpc_id            = aws_vpc.main.id
  cidr_block        = "10.40.10.0/24"
  availability_zone = "ap-south-1a"
  tags = { Name = "ai-dev-private-a" }
}
resource "aws_subnet" "private_b" {
  vpc_id            = aws_vpc.main.id
  cidr_block        = "10.40.20.0/24"
  availability_zone = "ap-south-1b"
  tags = { Name = "ai-dev-private-b" }
}
output "vpc_id" { value = aws_vpc.main.id }
output "private_subnet_ids" { value = [aws_subnet.private_a.id, aws_subnet.private_b.id] }

Line-by-line behavior: VPC creates an isolated IP space; DNS flags enable resolver and hostname behavior. Subnets divide CIDR into AZ-specific ranges. aws_vpc.main.id forms an implicit dependency. These are not yet routable production private subnets: routes, egress strategy, endpoints, security groups, EKS-required tags and additional AZ/operational resources are still required. AZ names can map differently between AWS accounts; prefer AZ IDs for cross-account consistency.

Where to run: engineer workstation or controlled CI agent with Terraform installed and short-lived AWS credentials.

aws sts get-caller-identity
terraform -chdir=terraform/environments/dev init
terraform -chdir=terraform/environments/dev fmt -check
terraform -chdir=terraform/environments/dev validate
terraform -chdir=terraform/environments/dev plan -out=tfplan
terraform -chdir=terraform/environments/dev show tfplan
terraform -chdir=terraform/environments/dev apply tfplan

Expected: plan lists VPC + two subnets + outputs; apply reports resource IDs. Save plan files securely: they may contain sensitive values. Never auto-approve production changes blindly.

27.3 Production design decisions

  • Separate AWS accounts for dev/stage/prod, distinct state keys, restricted deployment roles and SCP guardrails.
  • Module boundaries: network, eks, s3, iam; explicit typed inputs/outputs, no hidden cross-module coupling.
  • Prefer GitHub/Jenkins OIDC or STS role assumption to static AWS keys.
  • Use private EKS endpoints where operations support it; endpoint access and network routing must be designed together.
  • IAM policies should be resource-scoped; * is sometimes required for discovery APIs but justify every wildcard.
  • For secrets use Secrets Manager/SSM and workload identity; sensitive = true hides CLI output but does not encrypt Terraform state.

27.4 Incident: Terraform state lock stuck

Symptoms: Error acquiring the state lock. Diagnose: check active CI builds, CloudTrail/state object changes, backend connectivity, lock metadata. Avoid: deleting lock while another apply runs. Recovery: verify no active writer, preserve backup, use terraform force-unlock LOCK_ID only under approved incident procedure. Prevent: one deployment lane per environment, CI concurrency controls, short-lived sessions, S3 versioning.

Interview: Terraform plan aur apply ke beech resource change ho jaye? Answer: saved plan can become stale; provider may fail or report unexpected state. Re-plan after concurrent changes and never assume the saved plan is a universal transaction lock across AWS APIs.

Chapter 28 — Ansible production configuration

28.1 Agentless model

Ansible control node SSH/WinRM ke through remote hosts manage karta hai; default Linux SSH mode mein persistent agent install nahi hota. Inventory hosts define karta hai; playbook desired actions sequence karta hai; modules task execution perform karte hain. Idempotency ka matlab repeated run se unnecessary changes nahi hone chahiye. shell and command tasks carefully use karo, because they often lack automatic state checks.

28.2 Inventory and hardened Nginx playbook

File: ansible/inventories/prod/hosts.ini

[reverse_proxies]
proxy-01 ansible_host=10.40.10.11
proxy-02 ansible_host=10.40.20.11
[reverse_proxies:vars]
ansible_user=deploy
ansible_become=true

File: ansible/playbooks/nginx.yml

---
- name: Configure production reverse proxies
  hosts: reverse_proxies
  become: true
  serial: 1
  vars:
    nginx_package: nginx
  tasks:
    - name: Install Nginx
      ansible.builtin.package:
        name: "{{ nginx_package }}"
        state: present
    - name: Deploy proxy configuration
      ansible.builtin.template:
        src: ../templates/gateway.conf.j2
        dest: /etc/nginx/conf.d/gateway.conf
        owner: root
        group: root
        mode: '0644'
        validate: 'nginx -t -c /etc/nginx/nginx.conf'
      notify: Reload nginx
    - name: Ensure Nginx is enabled and running
      ansible.builtin.service:
        name: nginx
        enabled: true
        state: started
  handlers:
    - name: Reload nginx
      ansible.builtin.service:
        name: nginx
        state: reloaded

hosts targets inventory group; become privilege escalation; serial: 1 one-at-a-time change limits simultaneous outage risk; package package manager abstraction; template renders Jinja2; validate syntax checks candidate config before replace; notify triggers handler only on change. Note: template relative lookup paths and distro-specific Nginx include conventions must be tested in your repository and OS.

File: ansible/templates/gateway.conf.j2

upstream gateway_backend {
    server 127.0.0.1:8000;
    keepalive 16;
}
server {
    listen 8080;
    server_name _;
    location /healthz { return 200 'ok'; }
    location / {
        proxy_pass http://gateway_backend;
        proxy_set_header Host $host;
        proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-For $proxy_add_x_forwarded_for;
        proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-Proto $scheme;
        proxy_connect_timeout 5s;
        proxy_read_timeout 60s;
    }
}

This example is an internal HTTP hop, not an internet-facing TLS termination configuration. For production public exposure, terminate TLS at an approved ALB or configure TLS on Nginx with trusted certificates, HSTS after testing, access restrictions and rate limits. Do not trust forwarded client IP headers from arbitrary internet clients.

Run from control node:

ansible-inventory -i ansible/inventories/prod/hosts.ini --graph
ansible-playbook -i ansible/inventories/prod/hosts.ini ansible/playbooks/nginx.yml --check --diff
ansible-playbook -i ansible/inventories/prod/hosts.ini ansible/playbooks/nginx.yml

Security: SSH keys in managed secret store; use ansible-vault for encrypted vars but prefer dynamic retrieval from Secrets Manager for rotated secrets; no passwords in inventory; restrict sudo; protect --diff output from leaking secrets; pin collections.

Failure drill: one proxy reload fails → stop rollout due to serial: 1, inspect journalctl -u nginx, nginx -t, compare rendered template, restore last known good config. For HA, first remove host from load balancer, deploy, verify health, then re-add.

Chapter 29 — Helm: Kubernetes packaging and release engineering

29.1 Helm mechanism

Helm chart templates + values se Kubernetes manifests render karta hai. Helm release history cluster mein store hoti hai; helm upgrade manifests reconcile karta hai, but app readiness probes and safe schema migrations are separate concerns. Helm Kubernetes control plane ka replacement nahi hai: rendered resources API server ko submit hote hain, controllers desired state implement karte hain.

Directory:

charts/inference-gateway/
  Chart.yaml
  values.yaml
  templates/deployment.yaml
  templates/service.yaml

Chart.yaml

apiVersion: v2
name: inference-gateway
description: FastAPI inference gateway
version: 0.1.0
appVersion: "1.0.0"
type: application

values.yaml

replicaCount: 2
image:
  repository: 111122223333.dkr.ecr.ap-south-1.amazonaws.com/inference-gateway
  tag: "1.0.0"
resources:
  requests: {cpu: 200m, memory: 256Mi}
  limits: {cpu: 1, memory: 512Mi}
service:
  port: 80

templates/deployment.yaml

apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: {{ .Release.Name }}-gateway
spec:
  replicas: {{ .Values.replicaCount }}
  strategy:
    type: RollingUpdate
    rollingUpdate: {maxUnavailable: 0, maxSurge: 1}
  selector:
    matchLabels: {app: gateway, release: {{ .Release.Name }}}
  template:
    metadata:
      labels: {app: gateway, release: {{ .Release.Name }}}
    spec:
      containers:
        - name: gateway
          image: "{{ .Values.image.repository }}:{{ .Values.image.tag }}"
          ports: [{containerPort: 8000}]
          readinessProbe:
            httpGet: {path: /readyz, port: 8000}
            initialDelaySeconds: 5
            periodSeconds: 5
          livenessProbe:
            httpGet: {path: /healthz, port: 8000}
            initialDelaySeconds: 15
            periodSeconds: 10
          resources:
            {{- toYaml .Values.resources | nindent 12 }}

templates/service.yaml

apiVersion: v1
kind: Service
metadata:
  name: {{ .Release.Name }}-gateway
spec:
  type: ClusterIP
  selector: {app: gateway, release: {{ .Release.Name }}}
  ports: [{port: 80, targetPort: 8000}]

Why these fields: selectors must match pod labels; readiness excludes unready pods from Service endpoints; liveness triggers restarts when stuck; rolling strategy avoids voluntary unavailable replicas but does not protect against insufficient capacity or broken dependencies. Image tags are mutable: in production prefer digest-pinned images and admission controls. Add service account with AWS Pod Identity/IRSA, PDB, NetworkPolicy, topology spread, HPA and securityContext in a hardened chart.

Where to run: admin workstation/CI with kubeconfig and namespace-scoped permissions.

helm lint charts/inference-gateway
helm template gateway charts/inference-gateway --namespace ai
helm upgrade --install gateway charts/inference-gateway --namespace ai --create-namespace --atomic --wait --timeout 10m
kubectl -n ai rollout status deployment/gateway-gateway
helm history gateway -n ai
helm rollback gateway 1 -n ai

--atomic rolls back an unsuccessful upgrade; rollback cannot automatically reverse database migrations or external side effects. Use pre-deployment backup, backwards-compatible schema changes and separate migration jobs.

Incident: Pods pending → kubectl describe pod, inspect scheduling events (CPU, taints, topology), image pull auth, PVC bindings. helm status is not a substitute for application synthetic checks.

Chapter 30 — Nginx internals and production operations

30.1 Event-driven request processing

Nginx master process configuration read/reload and worker lifecycle manage karta hai; workers event loop use karke many sockets handle karte hain. Connection count ≠ concurrent active upstream requests; file descriptor limits, upstream keepalive, CPU, TLS overhead and worker connections capacity affect karte hain. proxy_pass URI rewrite behavior trailing slash ke saath change hota hai—routing tests mandatory hain.

TLS edge configuration (certificate paths supplied by certificate automation):

upstream api_backend {
    least_conn;
    server 10.40.10.101:8000 max_fails=3 fail_timeout=10s;
    server 10.40.20.101:8000 max_fails=3 fail_timeout=10s;
    keepalive 32;
}
server {
    listen 80;
    server_name api.example.com;
    return 301 https://$host$request_uri;
}
server {
    listen 443 ssl http2;
    server_name api.example.com;
    ssl_certificate /etc/ssl/certs/api.fullchain.pem;
    ssl_certificate_key /etc/ssl/private/api.key;
    ssl_protocols TLSv1.2 TLSv1.3;
    client_max_body_size 10m;
    location / {
        proxy_pass http://api_backend;
        proxy_http_version 1.1;
        proxy_set_header Connection "";
        proxy_set_header Host $host;
        proxy_set_header X-Request-ID $request_id;
        proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-Proto $scheme;
        proxy_connect_timeout 5s;
        proxy_read_timeout 60s;
    }
}

Production notes: Certificates need renewal automation and reload tests; real-client-IP trust configuration should allow only known load balancers; avoid emitting sensitive prompts in access logs; rate limits and WAF may be needed. least_conn chooses backend with fewer active connections, not least CPU/GPU utilization. Health behavior varies by Nginx edition and modules.

Runbook for 502: curl -vk https://api.example.com/healthz → check nginx -t → journalctl -u nginx → Nginx error log (connect() failed, upstream prematurely closed connection) → test backend socket curl http://10.40.10.101:8000/healthz → verify firewall/SG, upstream health, timeouts and backend process. 504: investigate latency, queue saturation, slow inference, DNS resolution, idle timeout alignment; increasing timeout alone hides capacity problems.

Chapter 31 — Apache HTTP Server production engineering

31.1 MPM and modules

Apache supports event, worker and prefork Multi-Processing Modules. Event MPM typically handles keepalive connections efficiently with threaded workers; prefork process model is useful for certain non-thread-safe legacy modules but consumes more memory. mod_proxy, mod_proxy_http, mod_ssl, mod_headers and mod_status each extend behavior. Select one MPM compatible with installed modules; benchmark before tuning.

Debian 12 example:

sudo apt-get update
sudo apt-get install -y apache2
sudo a2enmod proxy proxy_http ssl headers
sudo apache2ctl -M | grep -E 'mpm_|proxy|ssl'

Site: /etc/apache2/sites-available/api.conf

<VirtualHost *:443>
    ServerName api.example.com
    SSLEngine on
    SSLCertificateFile /etc/ssl/certs/api.fullchain.pem
    SSLCertificateKeyFile /etc/ssl/private/api.key
    ProxyPreserveHost On
    ProxyPass / http://127.0.0.1:8000/ connectiontimeout=5 timeout=60
    ProxyPassReverse / http://127.0.0.1:8000/
    RequestHeader set X-Forwarded-Proto "https"
    ErrorLog ${APACHE_LOG_DIR}/api-error.log
    CustomLog ${APACHE_LOG_DIR}/api-access.log combined
</VirtualHost>

Deploy and verify:

sudo a2ensite api.conf
sudo apache2ctl configtest
sudo systemctl reload apache2
curl -Ik https://api.example.com/

ProxyPass forwards requests, ProxyPassReverse rewrites selected response headers for reverse-proxy routing; it does not rewrite arbitrary HTML body content. ProxyPreserveHost sends original Host header; backend must validate allowed hosts. Production requires controlled TLS private-key permissions, certificate rotation, explicit HTTP redirect, security headers and backend access restrictions.

Incident: Apache 503 but backend healthy → inspect proxy:error, DNS/address, exhausted worker slots, connection pooling, SELinux/AppArmor constraints, proxy timeout, and host firewall. Check apache2ctl -S for vhost conflicts. Don't raise MaxRequestWorkers without estimating per-worker memory and OS limits.

Interview comparison: Nginx is event-driven by design; Apache event MPM is also efficient for many connections. Neither is universally faster. Choose based on module needs, workload benchmark, team expertise, operational visibility and integration.

Chapter 32 — NVIDIA GPU on Linux, AWS and EKS

32.1 Hardware/software dependency chain

GPU kernel driver host OS aur GPU hardware ke beech communication provide karta hai. CUDA user-space libraries GPU compute enable karti hain; cuDNN deep-learning kernels optimize karta hai; NCCL multi-GPU collective communication handle karta hai. Container image mein CUDA user-space libraries ho sakti hain, lekin host driver compatibility required hai. nvidia-smi driver/GPU state show karta hai; CUDA toolkit version aur driver-reported supported CUDA version identical hone ki zaroorat nahi.

Architecture:

PyTorch/vLLM container -> CUDA runtime libraries -> NVIDIA container runtime
                                              -> host NVIDIA driver -> GPU
Kubernetes scheduler -> nvidia.com/gpu extended resource advertised by device plugin
GPU Operator -> drivers/toolkit/device plugin/GFD/DCGM components (configuration-dependent)

32.2 Node validation

Run on GPU worker (SSH/SSM):

uname -r
lspci | grep -i nvidia
nvidia-smi
nvidia-smi --query-gpu=name,driver_version,memory.total,utilization.gpu,temperature.gpu --format=csv
journalctl -k | grep -Ei 'nvrm|nvidia|xid' | tail -30

Expected: GPU enumerated, driver loaded, memory/utilization metrics available. nvidia-smi failure can indicate driver/kernel mismatch, device not present, Secure Boot module issues, or hardware errors. Do not install host drivers manually over GPU Operator-managed drivers without a tested migration plan.

32.3 Kubernetes GPU pod

GPU nodes must have compatible driver/device plugin and advertised nvidia.com/gpu capacity. GPU Operator may install components, but EKS optimized AMIs can already contain drivers; avoid conflicting management modes.

apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata:
  name: gpu-smoke
  namespace: ai
spec:
  restartPolicy: Never
  nodeSelector:
    workload: gpu
  containers:
    - name: check
      image: nvcr.io/nvidia/cuda:12.4.1-base-ubuntu22.04
      command: ["bash", "-lc", "nvidia-smi"]
      resources:
        limits:
          nvidia.com/gpu: 1

Check:

kubectl get nodes -L workload
kubectl describe node GPU_NODE_NAME | grep -A8 Allocatable
kubectl apply -f gpu-smoke.yaml
kubectl -n ai describe pod gpu-smoke
kubectl -n ai logs gpu-smoke

nodeSelector matches labeled GPU pool; nvidia.com/gpu extended resource tells scheduler/device plugin to allocate one device; device sharing is not automatic. Pin CUDA image compatible with chosen driver; example image tag must be checked for registry availability before running. Production workloads need nonroot settings where feasible, image scanning, topology/taints, storage for model cache, node drain plan, and quotas.

32.4 MIG, utilization and observability

MIG supported GPU models ko hardware-isolated GPU instances mein partition kar sakta hai; availability and profiles depend on SKU and driver/operator setup. Time slicing offers sharing but not equivalent isolation. DCGM Exporter publishes GPU metrics for Prometheus (e.g. utilization, memory, temperature, power, ECC errors); label cardinality and tenant isolation carefully design karo. Measure tokens/sec, queue delay, p95 latency, GPU memory headroom and cost per 1M tokens, not only GPU utilization.

Incident — 0/5 nodes available: Insufficient nvidia.com/gpu: verify GPU node pool size, taints/tolerations, labels, device plugin pods, allocatable resources, GPU reservations, quotas, cluster autoscaler/Karpenter constraints and EC2 GPU quota/capacity. Incident — CUDA OOM: inspect model weight size, KV cache, context length, concurrent sequences, precision, tensor parallelism and fragmentation; choose safe batching and request limits before scaling.

Chapter 33 — MLflow production platform

33.1 Components and persistence

MLflow Tracking records runs, params, metrics, tags and artifacts. Backend store (often PostgreSQL) maintains metadata; artifact store (often S3) holds model files/plots. Registry manages named model versions and aliases. Metadata database backups and S3 versioning/retention are separate recovery concerns. Model registry entry is not itself a deployed inference service.

Reference architecture:

Training job -> HTTPS MLflow Tracking Server -> PostgreSQL (metadata)
                                |-> S3 (artifacts)
Model promotion -> registered model alias -> deployment pipeline -> serving endpoint

33.2 Deployment pattern

Provision PostgreSQL in private subnets (prefer managed RDS), S3 bucket with SSE-KMS, IAM role for MLflow server with narrowly scoped bucket access, and internal HTTPS ingress. Use Secrets Manager for DB credentials and Kubernetes Pod Identity/IRSA for S3; avoid embedding static keys. Configure authenticated ingress; MLflow must not be exposed openly to internet without access controls.

Example server command (run inside controlled MLflow container):

mlflow server \
  --host 0.0.0.0 --port 5000 \
  --backend-store-uri 'postgresql+psycopg2://mlflow:REPLACE@db.internal:5432/mlflow' \
  --artifacts-destination 's3://REPLACE-mlflow-artifacts/' \
  --serve-artifacts

The password placeholder is only illustrative: inject secret via environment or runtime secret file, and avoid printing connection URI in process listings/logs. Version-dependent MLflow CLI flags should be checked against pinned release. Use DB TLS verification and an application-specific least-privilege DB user.

Training client (Python):

import mlflow
from mlflow.models import infer_signature
from sklearn.datasets import load_iris
from sklearn.ensemble import RandomForestClassifier
from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split
from sklearn.metrics import accuracy_score

mlflow.set_tracking_uri("https://mlflow.internal.example.com")
mlflow.set_experiment("iris-classification")
X, y = load_iris(return_X_y=True, as_frame=True)
X_train, X_test, y_train, y_test = train_test_split(X, y, random_state=42)
with mlflow.start_run():
    model = RandomForestClassifier(n_estimators=100, random_state=42)
    model.fit(X_train, y_train)
    predictions = model.predict(X_test)
    mlflow.log_param("n_estimators", 100)
    mlflow.log_metric("accuracy", accuracy_score(y_test, predictions))
    signature = infer_signature(X_test, predictions)
    mlflow.sklearn.log_model(model, name="model", signature=signature)

What happens: client sends experiment/run metadata to tracking API; model serialization and artifact upload use server or client artifact routing according to configuration; registry promotion should require evaluation gates, security scan and approval. The name= model logging API is version-sensitive; pin/test MLflow and SDK versions together.

Incident — artifacts fail but metrics succeed: metadata DB is reachable; inspect artifact URI, IAM permissions (s3:PutObject, KMS permissions), bucket policy, endpoint DNS/routes, proxy size/timeouts, server/client artifact routing. Incident — tracking 500: inspect DB pool saturation, migration version, storage pressure, server logs and RDS connectivity. Backup/restore test both stores consistently.

Chapter 34 — Kubeflow Pipelines on Kubernetes

34.1 Architecture and responsibility

Kubeflow is a collection of Kubernetes-native ML capabilities; Kubeflow Pipelines (KFP) compiles a Python-defined workflow into an executable pipeline specification. Pipeline API/server, orchestration engine, artifact storage and metadata components work together. KFP component is a containerized unit with typed inputs/outputs; pipeline is DAG with dependencies. Running a KFP pipeline does not automatically train on GPUs: component resource requests, image, GPU node selectors and cluster permissions must be configured.

Pipeline lifecycle: Python DSL → compile pipeline spec → submit run → orchestrator creates task pods → task outputs/artifacts stored → run status/metadata surfaced → approval gate → model deployment.

34.2 Minimal KFP v2 pipeline

File: ml/pipeline.py

from kfp import dsl, compiler

@dsl.component(base_image="python:3.11-slim")
def validate_data(rows: int) -> str:
    if rows < 100:
        raise ValueError("Dataset too small for this example")
    return f"validated:{rows}"

@dsl.component(base_image="python:3.11-slim")
def train_stub(validation: str) -> str:
    print(f"Input: {validation}")
    return "training-completed-placeholder"

@dsl.pipeline(name="sample-ml-pipeline")
def sample_pipeline(rows: int = 200):
    checked = validate_data(rows=rows)
    train_stub(validation=checked.output)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    compiler.Compiler().compile(sample_pipeline, package_path="pipeline.yaml")

Run on developer/CI host:

python3 -m venv .venv
. .venv/bin/activate
pip install 'kfp>=2,<3'
python ml/pipeline.py

Expected: pipeline.yaml compiled. It is not yet submitted. KFP backend needs installation, authentication and an accessible API endpoint; pipeline upload/run steps depend on chosen Kubeflow distribution and auth mode. This example intentionally uses a training stub to isolate orchestration from real training. Replace it with versioned container images, pinned dependencies, input datasets, GPU requests and MLflow logging for production.

Operations: configure namespace isolation, RBAC/service accounts, Pod Security Standards, NetworkPolicies, artifact store credentials, resource quotas, pipeline concurrency and TTL/cleanup. Store training datasets and outputs with immutable/versioned identifiers; otherwise reproducibility breaks.

Incident — pipeline stuck Pending: kubectl describe pod for scheduler errors, PVC/storage, service account permissions, quota, GPU resources and image pulls. Incident — task passes but no artifact: check artifact store connectivity and pipeline component output contract. Incident — inconsistent results: verify input dataset version, image digest, seed, dependency lockfile and GPU nondeterminism.

Chapter 35 — End-to-end deployment, failure drills and interviews

35.1 Production release sequence

  1. Design: decide Bedrock vs self-hosted inference; document data classification, region availability, GPU costs, quotas and RTO/RPO.
  2. Terraform: bootstrap state, create network/subnets/routes/endpoints, IAM roles, S3, ECR, EKS and observability; review plan.
  3. Ansible: configure any EC2 reverse-proxy or utility hosts; do not use Ansible to imperatively change EKS objects already owned by Helm/GitOps.
  4. Jenkins: lint/test Python, run SAST/dependency/image scans, build signed image, push to ECR, record digest.
  5. Helm: deploy gateway and supporting services into EKS with readiness/liveness probes, quotas, PDB, autoscaling and workload identity.
  6. MLflow: initialize protected tracking server, DB migrations, artifact bucket and backups; test metrics + artifact write/read.
  7. Kubeflow: install compatible pipeline stack, compile/submit smoke workflow, validate metadata/artifact persistence and permissions.
  8. GPU: prepare supported AMI/driver/operator path, device plugin, node labels/taints, smoke GPU pod and DCGM telemetry.
  9. Inference: deploy approved model with vLLM or SageMaker; set concurrency/context/token limits; benchmark throughput and p95 latency.
  10. Go-live: Route 53 + ALB, TLS, WAF as appropriate, synthetic checks, alert routing, gradual traffic shift, rollback plan and runbooks.

35.2 Jenkins pipeline skeleton

pipeline {
  agent any
  options { timestamps(); disableConcurrentBuilds() }
  stages {
    stage('Validate') {
      steps {
        sh 'python3 -m compileall -q apps/gateway'
        sh 'helm lint charts/inference-gateway'
        sh 'terraform -chdir=terraform/environments/dev fmt -check'
      }
    }
    stage('Review deployment') {
      steps { input message: 'Approve controlled deployment after scans and plan review?' }
    }
    stage('Deploy to staging') {
      steps {
        sh 'helm upgrade --install gateway charts/inference-gateway -n ai --create-namespace --atomic --wait'
      }
    }
  }
}

This is a teaching skeleton, not complete production CI/CD. It omits image build/push, test coverage, IAM federation, immutable digests, artifact signing, scanning, promotion between accounts and production approvals. Do not use an unrestricted shared Jenkins agent with cluster-admin kubeconfig; deploy from isolated ephemeral agents with short-lived scoped identity.

35.3 Incident response method

0–5 min: determine blast radius, severity, change timeline; protect users via rollback/traffic shift if justified. 5–15 min: collect immutable evidence: request IDs, ALB target health, pod events, node status, GPU telemetry, CloudWatch logs, deployment history. 15–30 min: test one hypothesis at a time; restore service before deep root-cause work. After: postmortem with timeline, contributing factors, detection gaps, owner/action dates and game-day test.

Scenario A — ALB healthy targets but inference 504: Check actual target response latency, Nginx/ALB idle timeout alignment, upstream connection queue, GPU OOM/restarts, token generation duration, model cold start and client timeout. Distinguish first-token latency from total generation latency. Mitigate via queue limits, streaming, warm capacity or smaller model; raising timeouts is only a tactical choice.

Scenario B — MLflow server returns 503 after deployment: kubectl get pods,svc,endpointslices -n mlflow, check readiness, logs, DB connection pool, security group rules, secrets rotation and schema migrations. Roll back only if DB schema backward compatible.

Scenario C — GPU pods Pending during demand spike: compare kubectl describe pod scheduling reason, node pool capacity, EC2 quotas, Karpenter constraints, AZ capacity, GPU plugin health and model artifact prefetch time. Define queueing/fallback to Bedrock where permitted by policy.

35.4 Senior interview Q&A

Q1. Terraform vs Ansible vs Helm? Terraform owns cloud resource lifecycle through providers/state; Ansible configures hosts and performs procedural orchestration; Helm packages/version-controls Kubernetes manifests. Overlap is possible, but assign a single owner per resource to avoid configuration drift.

Q2. Why is a Kubernetes readiness probe different from liveness? Readiness controls whether Pod receives Service traffic; liveness restarts a container deemed unhealthy. A dependency outage should often fail readiness without forcing repeated liveness restarts.

Q3. GPU Operator and device plugin roles? Operator manages lifecycle of a compatible GPU software stack; device plugin advertises allocatable GPU extended resources and coordinates allocation. Driver/operator installation is configuration-dependent.

Q4. MLflow vs Kubeflow? MLflow manages experiment tracking, artifacts and registry lifecycle; Kubeflow Pipelines orchestrates multi-step ML workflows on Kubernetes. They complement rather than replace one another.

Q5. How to safely upgrade a Helm release with a DB migration? Make schema changes backward-compatible first, back up DB, migrate separately with observability, deploy new app via canary, verify metrics, and remove old columns only in a later release. Helm rollback alone does not undo database mutations.

Q6. Bedrock vs SageMaker vs EKS/vLLM? Bedrock offers managed foundation model inference; SageMaker provides managed ML training/hosting workflows; EKS/vLLM offers granular serving/runtime control with greater operational burden. Decide by model support, data residency, cost at load, GPU availability, latency, compliance and staffing.

35.5 Capstone acceptance criteria

  • Terraform plan reviewed, reproducible in a clean nonproduction account; no secrets in state outputs/logs.
  • HA networking and workload distribution validated across AZs; backup restore and RTO/RPO measured.
  • CI records tested image digest and immutable deployment provenance.
  • Nginx/Apache TLS and reverse-proxy behavior verified with integration tests.
  • MLflow tracks a run, stores and retrieves artifacts, and registry version is traceable to image/dataset.
  • Kubeflow pipeline succeeds, fails predictably on invalid inputs and exposes useful logs.
  • GPU smoke test succeeds; inference load test reports throughput, p50/p95 latency, memory and errors.
  • At least three controlled failure drills completed with written runbooks and postmortems.

Glossary

Artifact store: durable location for model binaries/plots. Backend store: MLflow metadata DB. CUDA: NVIDIA GPU compute platform. DCGM: NVIDIA Data Center GPU Manager. DAG: directed acyclic graph. Drift: difference between managed desired and actual infrastructure state. EKS: Elastic Kubernetes Service. Extended resource: Kubernetes scheduler-visible custom resource such as nvidia.com/gpu. Helm release: installed instance of a chart. Idempotency: repeated action converges to same desired result. MIG: Multi-Instance GPU. MPM: Apache Multi-Processing Module. RPO/RTO: recovery point/time objectives. SLO: service level objective. State: Terraform mapping of config to managed remote objects.

Follow pinned release documentation and organizational change control before deployment.

Never paste placeholder secrets into production. Validate service versions, IAM privileges, quotas, and organizational policies before executing. Production changes require peer review and rollback approval.
