Chapter 36 — Production Architecture and Engineering Decisions

36.1 Define service behavior before provisioning

Production ka matlab sirf kubectl apply success nahi. Define: service owner, traffic profile, data classification, availability target, RTO, RPO, incident severity, deployment frequency, cost ceiling, and compliance requirements. Example: public HTTPS API serving 100 requests/second peak; 99.9% monthly availability target; p95 latency < 500 ms for non-LLM routes; Bedrock requests have a separate latency SLO; RTO 60 minutes and RPO 15 minutes for the stateful metadata database. These are example objectives, not AWS guarantees.

Availability math: 99.9% over a 30-day month allows about 43.2 minutes unavailable. Distinguish uptime SLI (successful requests / eligible requests) from latency SLI (requests under threshold / eligible requests). Exclude only documented non-user-impacting traffic; do not hide outages by excluding all 5xx.

36.2 Multi-AZ reference diagram

Internet client
   | DNS query
Route 53 (alias record)
   | resolves
Public ALB + AWS WAF + ACM TLS
   | listener rules / target group
   +------ AZ-a ------------------- AZ-b ------+
   | private EKS worker nodes       private EKS worker nodes
   | FastAPI replicas (IRSA or EKS Pod Identity)
   | ClusterIP Service -> pod endpoints
   +------------------------------------------+
   |           |                   |
   |           +--> Bedrock Runtime (IAM + private VPC endpoint where supported)
   |           +--> SageMaker endpoint (IAM + VPC endpoint where supported)
   |           +--> S3 via gateway endpoint
   |           +--> RDS PostgreSQL Multi-AZ (private subnets)
   +--> CloudWatch logs/metrics; Prometheus/Grafana

CI: Git -> Jenkins -> tests/scan -> ECR -> Helm deploy -> smoke test
IaC: Terraform -> VPC, IAM, EKS, ALB prerequisites, S3
Host configuration: Ansible -> bastion/legacy VM (not EKS managed nodes)

Traffic path: Browser performs DNS resolution; client negotiates TLS with ALB; ALB evaluates listener rules, forwards to healthy targets; ingress controller reconciles ingress objects into ALB configuration when using AWS Load Balancer Controller. Service/EndpointSlice selection maps traffic to healthy application pods. In AWS Load Balancer Controller IP target mode, ALB targets pod IPs directly; in instance target mode, targets node ports. These are different paths—don't describe both as identical.

Failure domains: AZ failure should not remove all application replicas; set topology spread constraints and enough nodes in each AZ. RDS Multi-AZ supports failover but the application still needs connection retry/backoff. Regional failure requires a separately designed cross-region strategy; multi-AZ is not multi-region DR.

36.3 Choosing ECS, EKS, Fargate and Lambda

  • ECS Fargate: AWS-managed task compute; good for containerized stateless APIs without Kubernetes operational overhead. Fargate tasks need appropriate task execution role and separate task role.
  • EKS managed nodes: flexible K8s platform, GPU-capable EC2 nodes, Helm and ecosystem integration; cluster addons, upgrades, node OS and IAM need active operations.
  • EKS Fargate: serverless pod compute with feature restrictions; do not assume daemonsets or GPU device plugins run there.
  • Lambda: event-driven short-duration functions with service limits, cold starts, IAM and concurrency controls. Suitable for S3-triggered ingestion, not a universal substitute for GPU model serving.

Decision exercise: A FastAPI Bedrock proxy with modest traffic may start on ECS Fargate. A shared ML platform with GPU inference, KServe, and custom operators more naturally fits EKS on GPU EC2 nodes. Record trade-offs in an Architecture Decision Record (ADR).

Chapter 37 — Terraform: Reproducible AWS Networking

37.1 State and backend: why it matters

Terraform config (.tf) declares desired infrastructure; provider reads remote AWS APIs; state tracks object identity and attributes. terraform plan compares configuration, state, and observed resources, then proposes actions. State can contain sensitive values: encrypt it, tightly scope access, and never commit it to Git. S3 state locking via use_lockfile is supported in recent Terraform versions; DynamoDB-based locking is a legacy approach and should not be the default for new designs. Pin provider/module versions and review upgrade changes.

Bootstrap separation: Remote-state S3 bucket must exist before the backend can initialize. Provision it once in a separate bootstrap stack/account workflow with versioning, SSE-KMS where required, public access block, least-privilege access, and restricted deletion.

37.2 Repository

infra/
  bootstrap/                  # separate one-time backend setup
  modules/network/{main.tf,variables.tf,outputs.tf}
  envs/prod/{versions.tf,backend.tf,main.tf,variables.tf,prod.tfvars.example}
  policies/                  # policy-as-code rules

37.3 Backend configuration (envs/prod/backend.tf)

terraform {
  backend "s3" {
    bucket       = "REPLACE-unique-tf-state-bucket"
    key          = "platform/prod/terraform.tfstate"
    region       = "ap-south-1"
    encrypt      = true
    use_lockfile = true
  }
}

Explanation: bucket holds the state object; key separates environment path; region locates bucket; encrypt requests S3 server-side encryption; use_lockfile prevents concurrent writers when supported by the installed Terraform version. Encrypting the state does not replace IAM and KMS permissions. Configure bucket versioning and retention separately.

37.4 Provider and variables (versions.tf, variables.tf)

terraform {
  required_version = ">= 1.10, < 2.0"
  required_providers {
    aws = { source = "hashicorp/aws", version = "~> 6.0" }
  }
}
provider "aws" {
  region = var.aws_region
  default_tags { tags = { Project = "ai-platform", Environment = "prod", ManagedBy = "terraform" } }
}
variable "aws_region" { type = string; default = "ap-south-1" }
variable "vpc_cidr"   { type = string; default = "10.40.0.0/16" }

Why version constraints? Major provider upgrades can change defaults/schema; version pinning makes CI reproducible. Check current provider documentation and lockfile compatibility. Avoid hardcoded AWS keys: use role-based authentication via IAM Identity Center/STS or CI OIDC.

37.5 Network module (modules/network/main.tf)

data "aws_availability_zones" "available" { state = "available" }
resource "aws_vpc" "main" {
  cidr_block           = var.vpc_cidr
  enable_dns_support   = true
  enable_dns_hostnames = true
  tags = { Name = "prod-ai-vpc" }
}
resource "aws_internet_gateway" "igw" { vpc_id = aws_vpc.main.id }
resource "aws_subnet" "public" {
  count                   = 2
  vpc_id                  = aws_vpc.main.id
  cidr_block              = cidrsubnet(var.vpc_cidr, 8, count.index)
  availability_zone       = data.aws_availability_zones.available.names[count.index]
  map_public_ip_on_launch = false
  tags = { Name = "public-${count.index}", "kubernetes.io/role/elb" = "1" }
}
resource "aws_subnet" "private" {
  count             = 2
  vpc_id            = aws_vpc.main.id
  cidr_block        = cidrsubnet(var.vpc_cidr, 8, count.index + 10)
  availability_zone = data.aws_availability_zones.available.names[count.index]
  tags = { Name = "private-${count.index}", "kubernetes.io/role/internal-elb" = "1" }
}
resource "aws_eip" "nat" { count = 2; domain = "vpc"; depends_on = [aws_internet_gateway.igw] }
resource "aws_nat_gateway" "nat" {
  count         = 2
  allocation_id = aws_eip.nat[count.index].id
  subnet_id     = aws_subnet.public[count.index].id
  depends_on    = [aws_internet_gateway.igw]
}
resource "aws_route_table" "public" { vpc_id = aws_vpc.main.id }
resource "aws_route" "public_default" {
  route_table_id = aws_route_table.public.id
  destination_cidr_block = "0.0.0.0/0"
  gateway_id = aws_internet_gateway.igw.id
}
resource "aws_route_table_association" "public" {
  count = 2; subnet_id = aws_subnet.public[count.index].id
  route_table_id = aws_route_table.public.id
}
resource "aws_route_table" "private" { count = 2; vpc_id = aws_vpc.main.id }
resource "aws_route" "private_default" {
  count = 2
  route_table_id = aws_route_table.private[count.index].id
  destination_cidr_block = "0.0.0.0/0"
  nat_gateway_id = aws_nat_gateway.nat[count.index].id
}
resource "aws_route_table_association" "private" {
  count = 2; subnet_id = aws_subnet.private[count.index].id
  route_table_id = aws_route_table.private[count.index].id
}
resource "aws_vpc_endpoint" "s3" {
  vpc_id = aws_vpc.main.id
  service_name = "com.amazonaws.${var.aws_region}.s3"
  vpc_endpoint_type = "Gateway"
  route_table_ids = aws_route_table.private[*].id
}

Line-by-line mechanisms: cidrsubnet(/16,8,n) yields /24 subnets; two public subnets attach to one public route table with an IGW default route. Two private route tables each route internet-bound traffic to a NAT in the same AZ for improved AZ fault isolation. NAT gateways are billed even when idle. The S3 gateway endpoint adds S3 routes to private route tables, reducing reliance on NAT for supported S3 traffic. Public subnets are routable to the internet but instances without public IPs are not automatically publicly reachable. Route tables are not firewalls; apply SGs, NACLs and workload security controls separately.

Missing-by-design: This network module does not provision EKS, RDS, ALB, WAF, IAM, interface endpoints, IPv6, flow logs, or security groups; add reviewed modules for those resources before claiming production completion. EKS requires matching subnet/cluster tags and other prerequisites depending on controller setup.

37.6 Module interface

modules/network/variables.tf:

variable "vpc_cidr" { type = string }
variable "aws_region" { type = string }

modules/network/outputs.tf:

output "vpc_id" { value = aws_vpc.main.id }
output "private_subnet_ids" { value = aws_subnet.private[*].id }
output "public_subnet_ids" { value = aws_subnet.public[*].id }

envs/prod/main.tf:

module "network" {
  source = "../../modules/network"
  vpc_cidr = var.vpc_cidr
  aws_region = var.aws_region
}
output "private_subnet_ids" { value = module.network.private_subnet_ids }

37.7 Safe execution lab (Debian 12 admin workstation)

terraform version
aws sts get-caller-identity
cd infra/envs/prod
terraform fmt -check -recursive
terraform init
terraform validate
terraform plan -out=prod.tfplan
terraform show prod.tfplan
# Only after peer review / approval:
terraform apply prod.tfplan
terraform output private_subnet_ids

Expected observations: validate confirms schema/syntax, not that AWS permissions or quotas are sufficient; plan shows VPC, subnet, IGW, NAT, routes, S3 endpoint resources. apply prints resource IDs after provisioning. No change should be applied until plan is reviewed. Never terraform destroy production to clean up a lab. Use a separate disposable sandbox account.

37.8 Troubleshooting table

Symptom Evidence Likely cause Safe response
Error acquiring the state lock backend lock object, CI history parallel pipeline or abandoned run identify owner; avoid force-unlock until verified no active writer
NAT-created but no internet from pod route table, SG, DNS, NAT metrics route/DNS/SG/endpoint problem trace pod DNS -> node route -> NAT -> destination
AccessDenied during plan CloudTrail + caller identity missing IAM API permission scope required action; don't attach AdministratorAccess
drift after manual console change terraform plan -refresh-only unmanaged modification decide import/reconcile; don't blindly overwrite
subnet exhaustion free IP counts, CNI logs IP address planning add suitable subnets/secondary CIDR and revisit pod density

Chapter 38 — IAM, Trust, Kubernetes Workload Identity

38.1 Identity and authorization path

AWS evaluates whether a principal can perform an action on a resource, considering identity-based policies, resource policies, permission boundaries, session policies, SCPs, and explicit denies. IAM policies do not independently grant a principal the ability to assume any role: a role trust policy and caller-side permission conditions must also align. Explicit deny wins. sts:GetCallerIdentity confirms the effective principal, not all privileges.

38.2 Minimal Bedrock invocation policy

{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [{
    "Sid": "InvokeApprovedModel",
    "Effect": "Allow",
    "Action": ["bedrock:InvokeModel", "bedrock:InvokeModelWithResponseStream"],
    "Resource": ["arn:aws:bedrock:ap-south-1::foundation-model/REPLACE-MODEL-ID"]
  }]
}

Caution: Model/inference profile authorization varies by invocation type and region; check exact resource ARNs, model availability, cross-region inference profiles, and required permissions. Resource: * may sometimes be needed for specific operations, but must be justified and constrained.

38.3 Pod Identity vs IRSA

  • IRSA: Kubernetes service account token is projected; AWS STS AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity validates token against the cluster OIDC provider. IAM role trust policy limits service account and namespace.
  • EKS Pod Identity: EKS Pod Identity Agent and EKS association map service accounts to IAM roles using the pods.eks.amazonaws.com service principal. This avoids per-cluster OIDC trust configuration for supported scenarios. Confirm addon and platform compatibility.

Kubernetes service account (IRSA example):

apiVersion: v1
kind: ServiceAccount
metadata:
  name: ai-api
  namespace: ai-prod
  annotations:
    eks.amazonaws.com/role-arn: arn:aws:iam::123456789012:role/ai-prod-bedrock-invoke

This annotation is only for IRSA. The IAM role trust must match the service account subject system:serviceaccount:ai-prod:ai-api and the cluster OIDC audience. For Pod Identity, create an EKS Pod Identity association instead; do not assume the annotation alone is sufficient.

Verification from pod:

kubectl -n ai-prod exec deploy/ai-api -- python -c \
 'import boto3; print(boto3.client("sts").get_caller_identity()["Arn"])'

Expected: IAM role assumed for that workload, not worker node instance role. If the pod has no AWS CLI, Boto3 works if installed in the image. Never log temporary AWS credentials.

Failure drill: AccessDeniedException -> identify pod identity -> examine trust policy -> evaluate permissions and SCP -> verify Bedrock region/model entitlement -> reproduce minimal InvokeModel call -> audit CloudTrail. Don't broaden permissions as first response.

Chapter 39 — EKS, Kubernetes and Helm in Production

39.1 Control plane / data plane

EKS operates the managed Kubernetes control plane. Worker nodes run kubelet, container runtime, CNI networking and workload pods. A Deployment controller reconciles desired replicas to ReplicaSets; scheduler binds unscheduled pods to eligible nodes; kubelet ensures assigned containers are started; readiness controls endpoint eligibility, not container restart. Liveness failures can trigger restart; startup probes protect slow-starting workloads. Misconfigured probes can create self-inflicted outages.

39.2 Container build (app/Dockerfile)

FROM python:3.12-slim
ENV PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1 PYTHONUNBUFFERED=1
WORKDIR /app
COPY requirements.txt .
RUN pip install --no-cache-dir -r requirements.txt && useradd --uid 10001 --create-home appuser
COPY . .
USER 10001
EXPOSE 8080
CMD ["uvicorn", "main:app", "--host", "0.0.0.0", "--port", "8080"]

USER prevents default root execution. Pin dependencies with hashes for production, scan base images, and sign release artifacts. Container user IDs must match filesystem permissions. Expose is documentation, not an AWS firewall rule.

39.3 FastAPI readiness semantics (app/main.py)

from fastapi import FastAPI, HTTPException
import os
import boto3
from botocore.exceptions import ClientError, BotoCoreError
app = FastAPI()
region = os.environ.get("AWS_REGION", "ap-south-1")
model_id = os.environ.get("BEDROCK_MODEL_ID")
client = boto3.client("bedrock-runtime", region_name=region,
                      config=__import__('botocore.config', fromlist=['Config']).Config(
                          connect_timeout=3, read_timeout=60, retries={'mode': 'standard', 'max_attempts': 3}))
@app.get("/live")
def live(): return {"status": "alive"}
@app.get("/ready")
def ready():
    if not model_id:
        raise HTTPException(status_code=503, detail="model not configured")
    return {"status": "ready"}
@app.post("/generate")
def generate(payload: dict):
    prompt = payload.get("prompt", "")
    if not isinstance(prompt, str) or not 1 <= len(prompt) <= 4000:
        raise HTTPException(400, "prompt must be 1..4000 characters")
    try:
        response = client.converse(
            modelId=model_id,
            messages=[{"role":"user","content":[{"text":prompt}]}],
            inferenceConfig={"maxTokens":256, "temperature":0.2}
        )
        return {"text": response["output"]["message"]["content"][0].get("text", "")}
    except (ClientError, BotoCoreError):
        # Internal errors belong in structured, redacted logs; never leak raw provider details.
        raise HTTPException(503, "inference temporarily unavailable")

Design notes: Bedrock Converse supports compatible models, not every model; ensure model ID and region support. Readiness intentionally does not call Bedrock on every probe—external dependency probing can amplify throttling. Implement auth, request IDs, rate limits, structured logging, PII controls, response schema validation, and a proper circuit breaker before production exposure. This snippet is a teaching baseline, not a fully secured public API.

app/requirements.txt (example constraints; freeze tested versions before release):

fastapi>=0.115,<1
uvicorn>=0.30,<1
boto3>=1.35,<2

39.4 Helm chart structure

charts/ai-api/
  Chart.yaml
  values.yaml
  templates/deployment.yaml
  templates/service.yaml
  templates/serviceaccount.yaml
  templates/hpa.yaml
  templates/pdb.yaml

Chart.yaml:

apiVersion: v2
name: ai-api
version: 0.1.0
appVersion: "1.0.0"
type: application

values.yaml:

replicaCount: 3
image:
  repository: 123456789012.dkr.ecr.ap-south-1.amazonaws.com/ai-api
  digest: "sha256:REPLACE_WITH_REAL_DIGEST"
serviceAccount:
  name: ai-api
resources:
  requests: {cpu: 250m, memory: 256Mi}
  limits: {memory: 512Mi}
modelId: "REPLACE_WITH_SUPPORTED_MODEL_ID"

Why digest? A tag can be overwritten; an image digest pins content. A valid digest must come from the pushed ECR image. Helm templating does not itself grant permissions, create IAM roles, or guarantee cluster compatibility.

templates/deployment.yaml:

apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: {{ .Release.Name }}
spec:
  replicas: {{ .Values.replicaCount }}
  strategy:
    type: RollingUpdate
    rollingUpdate: {maxSurge: 1, maxUnavailable: 0}
  selector:
    matchLabels: {app: "{{ .Release.Name }}"}
  template:
    metadata:
      labels: {app: "{{ .Release.Name }}"}
    spec:
      serviceAccountName: {{ .Values.serviceAccount.name }}
      securityContext: {runAsNonRoot: true, runAsUser: 10001, seccompProfile: {type: RuntimeDefault}}
      topologySpreadConstraints:
        - maxSkew: 1
          topologyKey: topology.kubernetes.io/zone
          whenUnsatisfiable: ScheduleAnyway
          labelSelector:
            matchLabels: {app: "{{ .Release.Name }}"}
      containers:
        - name: api
          image: "{{ .Values.image.repository }}@{{ .Values.image.digest }}"
          ports: [{containerPort: 8080}]
          env:
            - name: BEDROCK_MODEL_ID
              value: {{ .Values.modelId | quote }}
          resources:
            {{- toYaml .Values.resources | nindent 12 }}
          readinessProbe:
            httpGet: {path: /ready, port: 8080}
            periodSeconds: 5
            failureThreshold: 3
          livenessProbe:
            httpGet: {path: /live, port: 8080}
            periodSeconds: 10
            failureThreshold: 3
          startupProbe:
            httpGet: {path: /live, port: 8080}
            periodSeconds: 5
            failureThreshold: 12
          securityContext:
            allowPrivilegeEscalation: false
            capabilities: {drop: [ALL]}
            readOnlyRootFilesystem: true

Key behavior: maxUnavailable:0 protects available replica count during a healthy rolling update but requires surge capacity. ScheduleAnyway is a soft AZ spread preference, not guaranteed placement; use DoNotSchedule only after checking node capacity and failure behavior. readOnlyRootFilesystem may require writable emptyDir mounts for libraries that write temporary files. Container requests drive scheduling; memory limit can cause OOMKilled. The service account must be created/associated separately.

templates/service.yaml:

apiVersion: v1
kind: Service
metadata: {name: "{{ .Release.Name }}"}
spec:
  type: ClusterIP
  selector: {app: "{{ .Release.Name }}"}
  ports: [{name: http, port: 80, targetPort: 8080}]

templates/serviceaccount.yaml:

apiVersion: v1
kind: ServiceAccount
metadata: {name: "{{ .Values.serviceAccount.name }}"}

templates/hpa.yaml:

apiVersion: autoscaling/v2
kind: HorizontalPodAutoscaler
metadata: {name: "{{ .Release.Name }}"}
spec:
  scaleTargetRef: {apiVersion: apps/v1, kind: Deployment, name: "{{ .Release.Name }}"}
  minReplicas: 3
  maxReplicas: 12
  metrics:
    - type: Resource
      resource:
        name: cpu
        target: {type: Utilization, averageUtilization: 65}

templates/pdb.yaml:

apiVersion: policy/v1
kind: PodDisruptionBudget
metadata: {name: "{{ .Release.Name }}"}
spec:
  minAvailable: 2
  selector: {matchLabels: {app: "{{ .Release.Name }}"}}

Production caveats: HPA needs a functioning metrics pipeline and realistic CPU requests; Bedrock-bound workloads may need custom metrics such as queue depth/concurrency. PDB applies to voluntary disruptions, not AZ failure. Add NetworkPolicy with a supported enforcing CNI, ingress configuration, Pod Identity/IRSA, and external secrets integration before rollout.

39.5 Render, deploy, verify, rollback

helm lint charts/ai-api
helm template ai-api charts/ai-api -n ai-prod > rendered.yaml
kubectl apply --dry-run=server -n ai-prod -f rendered.yaml
helm upgrade --install ai-api charts/ai-api -n ai-prod --create-namespace \
  --atomic --wait --timeout 10m
kubectl -n ai-prod rollout status deploy/ai-api --timeout=300s
kubectl -n ai-prod get pods -o wide
kubectl -n ai-prod get endpointslices -l kubernetes.io/service-name=ai-api
helm history ai-api -n ai-prod
# On approved rollback:
helm rollback ai-api <KNOWN_GOOD_REVISION> -n ai-prod --wait

Expected: Helm renders resource YAML, pods pass startup/readiness, EndpointSlices contain ready pod addresses, rollback uses an existing revision. Note --atomic may automatically roll back a failed upgrade; inspect Helm history/events to determine actual state. Never rollback database migrations blindly.

39.6 EKS incident: CrashLoopBackOff vs Pending vs ImagePullBackOff

kubectl -n ai-prod describe pod POD_NAME
kubectl -n ai-prod logs POD_NAME --previous --timestamps
kubectl -n ai-prod get events --sort-by=.lastTimestamp | tail -40
kubectl -n ai-prod top pods
kubectl get nodes -o wide
  • Pending: scheduler constraints, taints, resource requests, PV, IP exhaustion, node group scale-up.
  • ImagePullBackOff: wrong digest, ECR auth/network, missing ECR endpoints, repository permissions.
  • CrashLoopBackOff: process exits, missing env, invalid startup command, OOM, application bug. Inspect lastState.terminated.reason and exit code; don't assume memory issue without evidence.
  • Ready=False but Running: probe failure, app not listening on 0.0.0.0:8080, startup too slow, wrong path/port.

Chapter 40 — Jenkins: Controlled Software Supply Chain

40.1 Controller vs agents

Jenkins controller schedules jobs, stores configuration, coordinates credentials and plugins. Build jobs should run on isolated agents, ideally short-lived Kubernetes agents. Do not mount the Docker socket into untrusted jobs; it effectively grants host-level privileges. Prefer rootless/build-service approaches and approved image build tools. Limit credentials by environment and job; use ephemeral AWS role credentials through OIDC or workload identity rather than static keys.

40.2 Jenkinsfile with gates

pipeline {
  agent { label 'trusted-linux-build-agent' }
  options { timestamps(); timeout(time: 45, unit: 'MINUTES'); disableConcurrentBuilds() }
  environment {
    AWS_REGION = 'ap-south-1'
    ECR_REPO = '123456789012.dkr.ecr.ap-south-1.amazonaws.com/ai-api'
  }
  stages {
    stage('Checkout') { steps { checkout scm } }
    stage('Unit tests') {
      steps { sh 'python3 -m venv .venv && .venv/bin/pip install -r app/requirements.txt && .venv/bin/python -m compileall -q app' }
    }
    stage('IaC and Helm validation') {
      steps { sh 'terraform -chdir=infra/envs/prod fmt -check; helm lint charts/ai-api; helm template ai-api charts/ai-api > /tmp/rendered.yaml' }
    }
    stage('Security gates') {
      steps { sh './ci/run-approved-sast-sca-secret-and-image-scans.sh' }
    }
    stage('Build and publish') {
      steps { sh './ci/build-sign-push-and-write-digest.sh' }
    }
    stage('Production approval') {
      when { branch 'main' }
      steps { input message: 'Approve production deployment of tested immutable digest?' }
    }
    stage('Deploy') {
      when { branch 'main' }
      steps { sh './ci/deploy-verified-digest.sh' }
    }
    stage('Post-deploy validation') {
      when { branch 'main' }
      steps { sh './ci/smoke-and-slo-checks.sh' }
    }
  }
  post { always { archiveArtifacts artifacts: 'reports/**', allowEmptyArchive: true } }
}

Critical honesty: ci/*.sh scripts above are interfaces that your repository must implement, not magically executable commands. Real CI needs actual unit tests, SAST/SCA tools, SBOM, signature verification, image build tool, deployment identity, and environment promotion. Never replace missing scripts with echo success. input alone does not enforce separation of duties; configure Jenkins authorization and approvals.

40.3 Release invariants

Commit SHA -> immutable image digest -> signed artifact -> Helm values digest -> deployment record. A release should be reproducible, traceable, and reversible. Use protected branches and code review. Keep DB schema backward compatible during rolling updates; expand/migrate/contract instead of destructive changes before rollout.

40.4 Failure scenario

Symptom: Jenkins says SUCCESS but 20% of users receive 502. Investigate: ALB target health, application readiness, deployment ReplicaSets, pod events, ingress/controller reconciliation, AZ distribution, request IDs. Why pipeline can lie: it may only verify Helm exit code and not end-to-end traffic. Fix: add authenticated external smoke tests and post-deploy SLO monitoring with automatic or human-approved rollback policy.

Chapter 41 — Ansible, Nginx and Apache for Legacy/Edge Hosts

41.1 Why Ansible if Terraform exists?

Terraform provisions cloud resources; Ansible configures host software and desired state. Don't use Ansible to manually mutate EKS managed worker nodes; replace immutable nodes using node image/launch template lifecycle. Use Ansible for bastions, controlled legacy services, VM reverse proxies and OS baselines.

41.2 Inventory and playbook

inventory/production.ini:

[web]
web-a ansible_host=10.40.10.15
web-b ansible_host=10.40.11.15
[web:vars]
ansible_user=deployer
ansible_python_interpreter=/usr/bin/python3

playbooks/web.yml:

---
- name: Configure hardened reverse proxy
  hosts: web
  become: true
  serial: 1
  vars:
    nginx_upstream: "127.0.0.1:8080"
  tasks:
    - name: Install Nginx
      ansible.builtin.apt:
        name: nginx
        state: present
        update_cache: true
    - name: Deploy configuration
      ansible.builtin.template:
        src: ../templates/ai-api.conf.j2
        dest: /etc/nginx/sites-available/ai-api.conf
        owner: root
        group: root
        mode: '0644'
      notify: Validate and reload Nginx
    - name: Enable site
      ansible.builtin.file:
        src: /etc/nginx/sites-available/ai-api.conf
        dest: /etc/nginx/sites-enabled/ai-api.conf
        state: link
      notify: Validate and reload Nginx
  handlers:
    - name: Validate and reload Nginx
      ansible.builtin.shell: nginx -t && systemctl reload nginx
      changed_when: true

Explanation: serial:1 updates one web host at a time; become:true elevates for package/config tasks; template is idempotent when file content unchanged; handler runs only when notified. Shell validation prevents reload with invalid config but does not prevent an invalid config file from being written; for stronger safety, use a staging file and validate parameter on template (nginx -t -c requires full config context), or orchestrate with a separate preflight check. Avoid embedding passwords in playbooks; use Ansible Vault or external secrets manager and no_log: true on sensitive tasks.

41.3 Nginx reverse proxy template (templates/ai-api.conf.j2)

upstream ai_backend {
    server {{ nginx_upstream }};
    keepalive 16;
}
server {
    listen 8081;
    server_name _;
    access_log /var/log/nginx/ai-access.log;
    error_log /var/log/nginx/ai-error.log warn;
    location / {
        proxy_pass http://ai_backend;
        proxy_http_version 1.1;
        proxy_set_header Host $host;
        proxy_set_header X-Request-ID $request_id;
        proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-For $proxy_add_x_forwarded_for;
        proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-Proto $scheme;
        proxy_connect_timeout 3s;
        proxy_read_timeout 65s;
    }
}

TLS boundary: This example listens on internal HTTP 8081; terminate TLS at an ALB or add explicit Nginx TLS and certificate automation. Never expose plaintext reverse proxy on public internet without an approved design. Validate which X-Forwarded-* headers come from trusted proxy hops; don't trust arbitrary client-supplied values. Proxy timeouts must match ALB idle timeout and application inference behavior.

Verification:

ansible -i inventory/production.ini web -m ping
ansible-playbook -i inventory/production.ini playbooks/web.yml --check --diff
ansible-playbook -i inventory/production.ini playbooks/web.yml --limit web-a
ssh web-a 'sudo nginx -t && curl -fsS http://127.0.0.1:8081/live'

Expected pong, diff shows intended config changes, nginx -t successful, health endpoint returns JSON. Check-mode cannot perfectly predict all package/handler behavior.

41.4 Apache reverse proxy comparison

<VirtualHost *:8082>
    ServerName ai.internal.example
    ProxyPreserveHost On
    ProxyPass "/" "http://127.0.0.1:8080/" connectiontimeout=3 timeout=65
    ProxyPassReverse "/" "http://127.0.0.1:8080/"
    ErrorLog ${APACHE_LOG_DIR}/ai-error.log
    CustomLog ${APACHE_LOG_DIR}/ai-access.log combined
</VirtualHost>

Enable proxy, proxy_http, relevant MPM and required modules on Debian (a2enmod proxy proxy_http). Validate apachectl configtest, inspect apachectl -M, then systemctl reload apache2. Apache MPM event vs worker vs prefork affects concurrency/memory; avoid prefork without a specific module requirement. Nginx uses event-driven worker processes; both can serve as reverse proxies—choose based on team operations and requirements, not blanket claims that one is always faster.

Chapter 42 — Bedrock, SageMaker, Boto3 and Lambda Production Operations

42.1 Bedrock invocation lifecycle

Application authenticates through AWS credentials provider chain -> SigV4-signed request -> Bedrock Runtime authorizes IAM and model access -> provider/model inference -> response tokens and usage -> application enforces output policy. Bedrock abstracts model server patching and accelerator management, but you still own request authorization, prompts/data governance, quota planning, fallback strategy, observability, and application security.

Boto3 example with typed response checks:

import os
import boto3
from botocore.config import Config

client = boto3.client(
    "bedrock-runtime",
    region_name=os.environ["AWS_REGION"],
    config=Config(connect_timeout=3, read_timeout=120,
                  retries={"mode": "standard", "max_attempts": 4}),
)
response = client.converse(
    modelId=os.environ["BEDROCK_MODEL_ID"],
    messages=[{"role": "user", "content": [{"text": "Explain Kubernetes readiness probes."}]}],
    inferenceConfig={"maxTokens": 300, "temperature": 0.2},
)
print(response["output"]["message"]["content"][0]["text"])

Cost/latency: Track input/output token usage, requests per minute, error rates, throttling, p50/p95/p99 latency and timeout budgets. Retry only transient errors with bounded exponential backoff and jitter; don't retry all 4xx. Streaming improves perceived latency but needs client disconnect handling and abuse controls.

42.2 SageMaker model hosting lifecycle

Model artifact (S3) + inference container (ECR or supported framework image) -> SageMaker Model resource -> EndpointConfig (instance type/count, variants, data capture where applicable) -> Endpoint -> InvokeEndpoint. Separate control-plane deployment permissions from runtime invoke permissions. Inference containers must implement required health and invocation routes per hosting contract. SageMaker manages endpoint infrastructure, but customer controls VPC, KMS, image/model access, scaling policies, model behavior and cost.

import boto3, json
runtime = boto3.client("sagemaker-runtime", region_name="ap-south-1")
response = runtime.invoke_endpoint(
    EndpointName="REPLACE-EXISTING-ENDPOINT",
    ContentType="application/json",
    Body=json.dumps({"instances": [[1.0, 2.0, 3.0]]}).encode("utf-8"),
)
print(response["Body"].read().decode("utf-8"))

Important: Input schema is model/container-specific; the instances JSON above is an illustrative contract, not valid for every model. Do not assume SageMaker endpoints are public web servers; invocation uses authenticated AWS APIs.

42.3 Endpoint failure investigation

aws sagemaker describe-endpoint --endpoint-name YOUR_ENDPOINT --region ap-south-1
aws sagemaker describe-endpoint-config --endpoint-config-name YOUR_CONFIG --region ap-south-1
aws logs describe-log-groups --log-group-name-prefix /aws/sagemaker/Endpoints/YOUR_ENDPOINT

Inspect endpoint EndpointStatus and FailureReason, container CloudWatch logs, model artifact read permissions, ECR pull permissions, image architecture, health route behavior, container startup duration, memory/GPU OOM, and VPC connectivity. A successful API CreateEndpoint call only means creation was requested, not that endpoint became InService.

42.4 Lambda event-driven preprocessing

import json, os, boto3
s3 = boto3.client("s3")

def handler(event, context):
    for record in event.get("Records", []):
        if record.get("eventSource") != "aws:s3":
            continue
        bucket = record["s3"]["bucket"]["name"]
        from urllib.parse import unquote_plus
        key = unquote_plus(record["s3"]["object"]["key"])
        if not key.startswith("incoming/"):
            continue
        print(json.dumps({"event": "s3_object_received", "bucket": bucket, "key": key}))
    return {"statusCode": 200}

Safety: This code logs metadata and does not download or process objects. S3 event delivery can be duplicated/out of order; make processing idempotent using object version/ETag plus a durable idempotency store. Avoid recursion when writing back to a bucket that triggers the same Lambda. Configure concurrency, retries, DLQ/on-failure destinations as supported by trigger type, timeout, memory, IAM and logging retention.

Chapter 43 — NVIDIA GPU, MLflow, Kubeflow and LLM Serving

43.1 GPU dependency chain

GPU PCIe device -> kernel driver (nvidia modules) -> device files -> container runtime integration -> NVIDIA device plugin / GPU Operator -> Kubernetes allocatable nvidia.com/gpu -> scheduled pod -> CUDA runtime in container -> framework/model server. CUDA Toolkit inside image and host driver must be compatible; do not blindly install a host CUDA toolkit to fix every runtime mismatch.

GPU node checks:

lspci | grep -i nvidia
nvidia-smi
lsmod | grep nvidia
kubectl describe node GPU_NODE | grep -A10 -E 'Capacity:|Allocatable:'
kubectl -n gpu-operator get pods -o wide

nvidia-smi verifies driver/device visibility on host; node allocatable verifies Kubernetes resource advertisement. A host may see GPU while pod cannot because device plugin is unhealthy, runtime integration missing, taints block scheduling, or resource limits are wrong.

43.2 GPU pod YAML

apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata: {name: gpu-check, namespace: ml-prod}
spec:
  restartPolicy: Never
  nodeSelector:
    accelerator: nvidia
  tolerations:
    - key: nvidia.com/gpu
      operator: Exists
      effect: NoSchedule
  containers:
    - name: cuda-check
      image: nvcr.io/nvidia/cuda:12.4.1-base-ubuntu22.04
      command: ["nvidia-smi"]
      resources:
        limits:
          nvidia.com/gpu: 1

Prerequisites: GPU node labels and taints must actually exist, and the image must be accessible. Kubernetes GPU extended resources are typically set in limits; do not treat GPU as a fractionally shareable resource unless configured with supported MIG/time-slicing strategies. Time slicing and MIG provide different isolation/performance guarantees. Confirm CUDA image tag availability before use.

43.3 GPU monitoring and capacity

Use NVIDIA DCGM Exporter -> Prometheus -> Grafana for utilization, memory, ECC, temperature and power metrics, plus model-level tokens/s, request queue depth, p95 latency and OOMs. GPU utilization alone is not service capacity: a high VRAM model can OOM at low utilization; batching may raise throughput while harming tail latency. Estimate capacity by load testing with representative context length and concurrency. For distributed training, examine NCCL, network topology, EFA where applicable, collective communication, and node placement.

43.4 MLflow production persistence

MLflow tracking server stores run metadata in a SQL backend (e.g. RDS PostgreSQL) and artifacts in S3. The model registry relies on supported backend store capabilities. Production requires TLS, private networking, IAM, backup, authentication/authorization, database migrations, log retention and artifact lifecycle policy. Never expose an unauthenticated tracking server publicly.

# Demonstration only: execute on an authorized host with network access to RDS.
export MLFLOW_BACKEND_STORE_URI='postgresql+psycopg2://USER:PASSWORD@RDS_HOST:5432/mlflow'
export MLFLOW_ARTIFACT_ROOT='s3://REPLACE-MLFLOW-ARTIFACT-BUCKET/'
mlflow server \
  --backend-store-uri "$MLFLOW_BACKEND_STORE_URI" \
  --default-artifact-root "$MLFLOW_ARTIFACT_ROOT" \
  --host 127.0.0.1 --port 5000

Warning: Environment variables and process arguments can leak credentials. Use Secrets Manager/CSI/external secrets and IAM roles in actual production; enforce HTTPS and auth through a trusted ingress. This command binds localhost intentionally. Back up both PostgreSQL and S3 artifacts; backing up only one leaves incomplete experiments.

43.5 MLflow Python example

import mlflow
mlflow.set_tracking_uri("https://mlflow.internal.example")
mlflow.set_experiment("fraud-classifier")
with mlflow.start_run(run_name="baseline-v1"):
    mlflow.log_param("algorithm", "logistic-regression")
    mlflow.log_metric("validation_f1", 0.81)  # illustrative measured value
    mlflow.set_tag("git_sha", "REPLACE_COMMIT_SHA")

Real training must compute metrics from validation data; the 0.81 above is explicitly illustrative. Record dataset version, feature schema, model signature, environment, commit and approval state for reproducibility.

43.6 Kubeflow Pipelines: orchestrate steps, not magic training

from kfp import dsl, compiler

@dsl.component(base_image="python:3.11-slim")
def validate_dataset(rows: int) -> str:
    if rows < 100:
        raise ValueError("Dataset too small")
    return "validated"

@dsl.component(base_image="python:3.11-slim")
def train_dummy(validation: str) -> float:
    assert validation == "validated"
    return 0.81  # placeholder; replace with real training/evaluation

@dsl.pipeline(name="validated-model-training")
def pipeline(rows: int = 1000):
    validated = validate_dataset(rows=rows)
    train_dummy(validation=validated.output)

compiler.Compiler().compile(pipeline_func=pipeline, package_path="pipeline.yaml")

Mechanism: KFP compiler turns the Python DSL graph into pipeline specification; backend orchestrates tasks as Kubernetes workloads through its installed components. Each task image and dependency must be accessible. Configure pipeline service account, artifact storage, execution isolation, retries, caching and resource requests. This code is a control-flow demonstration; it does not train a model.

43.7 vLLM and KServe production checklist

  • Validate model license and weight access, model size, GPU VRAM, tensor-parallel topology, quantization support, and context-length budget.
  • Build immutable serving images; load models from approved S3/object storage or internal registry; no internet dependency during startup if air-gapped.
  • Configure readiness only after model weights loaded; set startup probe budget for cold start; preserve request draining on rolling updates.
  • Benchmark time to first token, tokens/sec, concurrency, p95/p99, GPU memory and OOM behavior; configure queue limits and overload response.
  • Separate inference from training node pools; use taints, tolerations, node affinity, PodDisruptionBudgets and capacity reservations.
  • Monitor model-serving errors, prompt token counts and costs; redact sensitive prompts in logs.

Chapter 44 — Observability, Incident Response, DR

44.1 Golden signals

Latency (p50/p95/p99), traffic (requests/s, tokens/s), errors (5xx, failed inferences), saturation (CPU, memory, GPU, queue depth, NAT connections). Metrics tell what; logs and traces help explain why. Propagate correlation IDs from ALB/API through FastAPI and downstream AWS calls. Never store raw secrets, access tokens or sensitive prompts in unrestricted logs.

44.2 CloudWatch and Kubernetes checks

aws cloudwatch list-metrics --namespace AWS/ApplicationELB --region ap-south-1
aws logs describe-log-groups --log-group-name-prefix /aws/eks/ --region ap-south-1
kubectl -n ai-prod get deploy,pods,svc,hpa,pdb
kubectl -n ai-prod top pods
kubectl -n ai-prod get events --sort-by=.lastTimestamp | tail -30

Caution: list-metrics may omit inactive metrics; check actual dimensions and periods. CloudWatch log groups depend on enabled log shipping/control plane logging. kubectl top needs Metrics Server or compatible metrics API.

44.3 Incident: ALB 502 after rollout

  1. Triage: scope, start time, affected AZs/routes, recent deployments; freeze further rollout.
  2. Evidence: ALB HTTPCode_ELB_5XX_Count vs target 5xx, target health reason, access logs, ingress controller logs, pod readiness, EndpointSlices.
  3. Hypotheses: target port mismatch, TLS backend protocol mismatch, unhealthy pods, target deregistration timing, connection reset, security group rules.
  4. Mitigate: shift traffic to last known-good target group/deployment, or helm rollback after validating database compatibility.
  5. Verify: synthetic request, user-facing SLI, target health stable across AZs.
  6. Prevent: add preflight ingress checks, graceful shutdown, proper terminationGracePeriodSeconds, release canary and post-deploy monitoring.

44.4 Incident: GPU pod Pending

kubectl -n ml-prod describe pod gpu-check
kubectl describe node GPU_NODE
kubectl -n gpu-operator get pods
kubectl get nodes -L accelerator

Interpret scheduler events: insufficient nvidia.com/gpu vs taint mismatch vs affinity mismatch vs node autoscaler failure. nvidia-smi on node is not enough to prove K8s resource advertisement. For driver mismatch inspect operator logs, kernel version and supported driver/CUDA matrix. Do not reboot all GPU nodes at once.

44.5 Backups and DR

  • RDS: automated backups, PITR settings, cross-region copy if needed; test restores in isolated environment. Record RPO from backup/replication design, not assumptions.
  • S3: versioning, object lock where required, lifecycle, replication, backup controls and IAM recovery. Replication is not a substitute for deletion protection and backup.
  • EKS: declarative manifests/Helm in Git, Terraform state backup, etcd control plane managed by EKS; back up persistent volumes and application data separately. Don't assume a YAML export recovers a database.
  • MLflow: restore both metadata DB and artifact objects; validate experiment/model registry references after restore.
  • Secrets: recovery procedures for KMS keys, secrets manager, certificates and OIDC dependencies; avoid backup that exposes plaintext secrets.

DR tabletop exercise: Simulate AZ outage, RDS failover, compromised CI credentials, deleted S3 artifact, and corrupted model release. For each: define detection, containment, escalation, RTO clock start, recovery evidence and postmortem actions.

Chapter 45 — Capstone, Acceptance Criteria and Senior Interview Drills

45.1 Capstone project: AI Inference Platform

Deliverables: Git repo with Terraform network/EKS/IAM modules, CI pipeline, immutable ECR image, Helm chart, FastAPI Bedrock API, SageMaker invocation integration, optional GPU vLLM path, MLflow tracking, Kubeflow pipeline, monitoring dashboards, security controls, ADRs, runbooks, cost estimate and disaster-recovery evidence.

Acceptance gates (all required before calling it production-ready):

  1. Two AZs, multiple healthy application replicas, tested disruption and node drain.
  2. Private workloads, least-privilege IAM, reviewed outbound access and endpoints, TLS and encryption.
  3. Secret rotation, vulnerability scanning, image provenance, dependency pinning, signed artifacts.
  4. SLO dashboard, alerts, log retention, traces and redaction.
  5. Load test at agreed concurrency, failure-injection and graceful degradation.
  6. Tested restore of stateful dependencies and documented RTO/RPO results.
  7. Release approval, immutable artifacts, smoke tests, rollback and DB migration compatibility.
  8. Terraform plan approval and drift detection; budget alarms and tagging.
  9. Bedrock/SageMaker model availability, quotas and inference profiles validated in target region.
  10. Operational ownership: on-call, escalation, incident communications and runbooks.

45.2 Interview questions with reasoning

Q1: Why not deploy every service to EKS? A: EKS provides Kubernetes ecosystem and GPU flexibility but creates cluster operations, addon upgrades, node scaling and security responsibilities. ECS Fargate can reduce overhead for simpler stateless services. Decide by workload and team capability, not trend.

Q2: How does an EKS pod access S3 without static keys? A: Bind Kubernetes service account to scoped AWS role through EKS Pod Identity or IRSA; Boto3 credential chain receives temporary credentials. IAM policy grants bucket actions; network path uses S3 gateway endpoint where suitable. Verify STS principal and CloudTrail. A service account by itself doesn't grant S3 permissions.

Q3: Terraform plan is clean but application is down. Why? A: Terraform validates desired infrastructure resources, not live application SLOs. App bug, deployment, data, TLS, DNS, or runtime quota can fail without IaC drift. Correlate infra state with service telemetry and release timeline.

Q4: GPU available on EC2 but Kubernetes says insufficient GPU. A: Verify device plugin/GPU Operator, runtime configuration, node allocatable, taints/labels, resource requests, MIG partitioning and scheduler events. Host driver health and K8s advertisement are separate checks.

Q5: Bedrock vs SageMaker vs self-hosted vLLM? A: Bedrock offers managed foundation-model inference and simpler operational surface. SageMaker gives managed model hosting for supported custom ML models and inference configurations. Self-hosted vLLM gives more control over model runtime/hardware but shifts GPU capacity, patching, autoscaling, reliability and model lifecycle responsibility to your team. Evaluate governance, model requirements, performance and total cost.

Q6: What does helm --atomic protect against? A: It rolls back an unsuccessful upgrade/release operation according to Helm behavior. It cannot reverse irreversible DB schema changes, external side effects, S3 mutations or corrupted downstream data. Release architecture must handle those separately.

Q7: How would you debug ALB 504? A: Check ALB metrics/access logs and target response time, backend timeouts, network reachability, target health, pod CPU/queue and inference downstream latency. Determine whether ALB waited for an unresponsive target versus upstream timeout. Tune only after identifying bottleneck; don't blindly increase timeouts.

Q8: How do you make MLflow recoverable? A: Separate metadata DB and artifacts, back up both, protect KMS and IAM dependencies, restore into test environment, verify experiment/run/model references and measure actual recovery time/data loss.

45.3 Practical exam

Scenario: A production model release raises p95 latency and GPU OOM errors after a Jenkins deploy. Candidate must identify new model VRAM footprint, batching/context length, GPU metrics, canary comparison, immutable release digest, safe rollback, customer impact, and preventive load test. Evaluate diagnosis based on evidence—not memorized commands.

45.4 Further deep-dive volumes required

To reach encyclopedic depth, additional dedicated volumes should expand: Linux kernel and networking packet traces; AWS Organizations/SCP/landing zones; IAM policy evaluation labs; Terraform module testing and policy as code; EKS installation/upgrade/addons/CNI/CSI; Jenkins controller/agent security; full SageMaker pipelines and distributed training; Bedrock Agents/Knowledge Bases/Guardrails; GPU Operator/MIG/NCCL/TensorRT-LLM; MLflow model registry and deployment; Kubeflow multi-tenant installation and operations; threat modeling, compliance and regional DR. This volume does not claim to replace those.

Glossary

  • ALB: Application Load Balancer, Layer-7 HTTP/HTTPS routing.
  • AZ: Availability Zone, isolated datacenter grouping within an AWS region.
  • CNI: Container Network Interface, plugin model for pod networking.
  • CSI: Container Storage Interface, plugin model for persistent storage.
  • HPA: Horizontal Pod Autoscaler, replica scaling controller.
  • IRSA: IAM Roles for Service Accounts on EKS.
  • MIG: Multi-Instance GPU, supported NVIDIA hardware GPU partitioning.
  • PDB: PodDisruptionBudget, limits voluntary disruption.
  • RPO: Recovery Point Objective, acceptable data loss window.
  • RTO: Recovery Time Objective, targeted service restoration time.
  • SLO: Service Level Objective, reliability target based on SLI.
  • STS: AWS Security Token Service, temporary credentials.

Volume 4 — Senior Production Interview & Incident Engineering Workbook

Language: Hinglish | Audience: Senior Infrastructure / AWS DevOps / SRE / MLOps | Edition: October 2026

Integrity note: Ye study workbook hai, interview guarantee nahi. Examples are illustrative, not tested against your AWS account. Production mein change approval, backups, least privilege aur non-production validation mandatory hain. Commands with placeholders require adaptation. Some incidents are simulated rather than actual customer postmortems.

Learning contract

Har question ka jawab WHAT → WHY → INTERNAL FLOW → FAILURE → VERIFICATION → TRADEOFF mein do. Interview mein sirf command bolna sufficient nahi. Har scenario ke liye impact, blast radius, timeline, evidence, mitigation, recovery and prevention cover karo. Labs ko isolated account/cluster mein execute karo. Do not deliberately break production.

Production incident simulation 1

Situation: Linux CPU scheduling and processes se related deployment ke turant baad error rate badh gaya, users impacted, monitoring partial hai.

  1. Triage: incident severity declare karo, change freeze, ownership assign, exact impact window record karo.
  2. Scope: affected region/AZ/node/namespace/endpoint identify karo; working vs failing paths compare karo.
  3. Evidence: recent release, IAM changes, DNS, network path, health checks, logs, saturation, quotas, dependencies inspect karo.
  4. Mitigation: last known-good config par rollback ya traffic diversion only after blast-radius review; risky deletion avoid karo.
  5. Verification: synthetic transaction, p50/p95/p99, 5xx, error budget, business KPI compare karo.
  6. Postmortem: timeline, triggering change, contributing factors, detection gaps, actionable prevention and owner/date.

Interview drill: 90 seconds mein incident ka initial response bolo; 5 minutes mein root-cause tree draw karo; 10 minutes mein rollback and validation plan present karo.

Hands-on lab 1 — controlled validation

Goal: Linux CPU scheduling and processes ka architecture diagram banao, baseline collect karo, ek configuration change non-production mein apply karo, failure signal observe karo, rollback verify karo.

Deliverables: README, redacted logs, diagram, change diff, rollback notes, 5 interview answers, cost and security checklist.

Pass criteria: pre/post baseline available; rollback repeatable; no embedded credentials; all changes traceable; expected behavior and actual observation separately documented.

Production incident simulation 3

Situation: Linux networking, DNS and TLS se related deployment ke turant baad error rate badh gaya, users impacted, monitoring partial hai.

  1. Triage: incident severity declare karo, change freeze, ownership assign, exact impact window record karo.

  3. Evidence: recent release, IAM changes, DNS, network path, health checks, logs, saturation, quotas, dependencies inspect karo.
  4. Mitigation: last known-good config par rollback ya traffic diversion only after blast-radius review; risky deletion avoid karo.
  5. Verification: synthetic transaction, p50/p95/p99, 5xx, error budget, business KPI compare karo.
  6. Postmortem: timeline, triggering change, contributing factors, detection gaps, actionable prevention and owner/date.

Interview drill: 90 seconds mein incident ka initial response bolo; 5 minutes mein root-cause tree draw karo; 10 minutes mein rollback and validation plan present karo.

Production incident simulation 7

Situation: AWS S3 encryption lifecycle replication se related deployment ke turant baad error rate badh gaya, users impacted, monitoring partial hai.

  1. Triage: incident severity declare karo, change freeze, ownership assign, exact impact window record karo.
  2. Scope: affected region/AZ/node/namespace/endpoint identify karo; working vs failing paths compare karo.
  3. Evidence: recent release, IAM changes, DNS, network path, health checks, logs, saturation, quotas, dependencies inspect karo.
  4. Mitigation: last known-good config par rollback ya traffic diversion only after blast-radius review; risky deletion avoid karo.
  5. Verification: synthetic transaction, p50/p95/p99, 5xx, error budget, business KPI compare karo.
  6. Postmortem: timeline, triggering change, contributing factors, detection gaps, actionable prevention and owner/date.

Interview drill: 90 seconds mein incident ka initial response bolo; 5 minutes mein root-cause tree draw karo; 10 minutes mein rollback and validation plan present karo.

Hands-on lab 7 — controlled validation

Goal: AWS S3 encryption lifecycle replication ka architecture diagram banao, baseline collect karo, ek configuration change non-production mein apply karo, failure signal observe karo, rollback verify karo.

Deliverables: README, redacted logs, diagram, change diff, rollback notes, 5 interview answers, cost and security checklist.

Pass criteria: pre/post baseline available; rollback repeatable; no embedded credentials; all changes traceable; expected behavior and actual observation separately documented.

Production incident simulation 11

Situation: ECS Fargate deployments se related deployment ke turant baad error rate badh gaya, users impacted, monitoring partial hai.

  1. Triage: incident severity declare karo, change freeze, ownership assign, exact impact window record karo.
  2. Scope: affected region/AZ/node/namespace/endpoint identify karo; working vs failing paths compare karo.
  3. Evidence: recent release, IAM changes, DNS, network path, health checks, logs, saturation, quotas, dependencies inspect karo.
  4. Mitigation: last known-good config par rollback ya traffic diversion only after blast-radius review; risky deletion avoid karo.
  5. Verification: synthetic transaction, p50/p95/p99, 5xx, error budget, business KPI compare karo.
  6. Postmortem: timeline, triggering change, contributing factors, detection gaps, actionable prevention and owner/date.

Interview drill: 90 seconds mein incident ka initial response bolo; 5 minutes mein root-cause tree draw karo; 10 minutes mein rollback and validation plan present karo.

Hands-on lab 11 — controlled validation

Goal: ECS Fargate deployments ka architecture diagram banao, baseline collect karo, ek configuration change non-production mein apply karo, failure signal observe karo, rollback verify karo.

Deliverables: README, redacted logs, diagram, change diff, rollback notes, 5 interview answers, cost and security checklist.

Pass criteria: pre/post baseline available; rollback repeatable; no embedded credentials; all changes traceable; expected behavior and actual observation separately documented.

Production incident simulation 15

Situation: Kubernetes PV PVC CSI se related deployment ke turant baad error rate badh gaya, users impacted, monitoring partial hai.

  1. Triage: incident severity declare karo, change freeze, ownership assign, exact impact window record karo.
  2. Scope: affected region/AZ/node/namespace/endpoint identify karo; working vs failing paths compare karo.
  3. Evidence: recent release, IAM changes, DNS, network path, health checks, logs, saturation, quotas, dependencies inspect karo.
  4. Mitigation: last known-good config par rollback ya traffic diversion only after blast-radius review; risky deletion avoid karo.
  5. Verification: synthetic transaction, p50/p95/p99, 5xx, error budget, business KPI compare karo.
  6. Postmortem: timeline, triggering change, contributing factors, detection gaps, actionable prevention and owner/date.

Interview drill: 90 seconds mein incident ka initial response bolo; 5 minutes mein root-cause tree draw karo; 10 minutes mein rollback and validation plan present karo.

Production incident simulation 19

Situation: Ansible inventory roles Vault se related deployment ke turant baad error rate badh gaya, users impacted, monitoring partial hai.

  3. Evidence: recent release, IAM changes, DNS, network path, health checks, logs, saturation, quotas, dependencies inspect karo.
  4. Mitigation: last known-good config par rollback ya traffic diversion only after blast-radius review; risky deletion avoid karo.
  5. Verification: synthetic transaction, p50/p95/p99, 5xx, error budget, business KPI compare karo.
  6. Postmortem: timeline, triggering change, contributing factors, detection gaps, actionable prevention and owner/date.

Interview drill: 90 seconds mein incident ka initial response bolo; 5 minutes mein root-cause tree draw karo; 10 minutes mein rollback and validation plan present karo.

  3. Evidence: recent release, IAM changes, DNS, network path, health checks, logs, saturation, quotas, dependencies inspect karo.
  4. Mitigation: last known-good config par rollback ya traffic diversion only after blast-radius review; risky deletion avoid karo.
  5. Verification: synthetic transaction, p50/p95/p99, 5xx, error budget, business KPI compare karo.
  6. Postmortem: timeline, triggering change, contributing factors, detection gaps, actionable prevention and owner/date.

Interview drill: 90 seconds mein incident ka initial response bolo; 5 minutes mein root-cause tree draw karo; 10 minutes mein rollback and validation plan present karo.

Production incident simulation 40

Situation: GitOps Argo CD se related deployment ke turant baad error rate badh gaya, users impacted, monitoring partial hai.

  1. Triage: incident severity declare karo, change freeze, ownership assign, exact impact window record karo.
  2. Scope: affected region/AZ/node/namespace/endpoint identify karo; working vs failing paths compare karo.
  3. Evidence: recent release, IAM changes, DNS, network path, health checks, logs, saturation, quotas, dependencies inspect karo.
  4. Mitigation: last known-good config par rollback ya traffic diversion only after blast-radius review; risky deletion avoid karo.
  5. Verification: synthetic transaction, p50/p95/p99, 5xx, error budget, business KPI compare karo.
  6. Postmortem: timeline, triggering change, contributing factors, detection gaps, actionable prevention and owner/date.

Interview drill: 90 seconds mein incident ka initial response bolo; 5 minutes mein root-cause tree draw karo; 10 minutes mein rollback and validation plan present karo.

Hands-on lab 40 — controlled validation

Goal: GitOps Argo CD ka architecture diagram banao, baseline collect karo, ek configuration change non-production mein apply karo, failure signal observe karo, rollback verify karo.

Deliverables: README, redacted logs, diagram, change diff, rollback notes, 5 interview answers, cost and security checklist.

Pass criteria: pre/post baseline available; rollback repeatable; no embedded credentials; all changes traceable; expected behavior and actual observation separately documented.

Production incident simulation 44

Situation: ML pipeline promotion rollback se related deployment ke turant baad error rate badh gaya, users impacted, monitoring partial hai.

  1. Triage: incident severity declare karo, change freeze, ownership assign, exact impact window record karo.
  2. Scope: affected region/AZ/node/namespace/endpoint identify karo; working vs failing paths compare karo.
  3. Evidence: recent release, IAM changes, DNS, network path, health checks, logs, saturation, quotas, dependencies inspect karo.
  4. Mitigation: last known-good config par rollback ya traffic diversion only after blast-radius review; risky deletion avoid karo.
  5. Verification: synthetic transaction, p50/p95/p99, 5xx, error budget, business KPI compare karo.
  6. Postmortem: timeline, triggering change, contributing factors, detection gaps, actionable prevention and owner/date.

Interview drill: 90 seconds mein incident ka initial response bolo; 5 minutes mein root-cause tree draw karo; 10 minutes mein rollback and validation plan present karo.

Hands-on lab 44 — controlled validation

Goal: ML pipeline promotion rollback ka architecture diagram banao, baseline collect karo, ek configuration change non-production mein apply karo, failure signal observe karo, rollback verify karo.

Deliverables: README, redacted logs, diagram, change diff, rollback notes, 5 interview answers, cost and security checklist.

Pass criteria: pre/post baseline available; rollback repeatable; no embedded credentials; all changes traceable; expected behavior and actual observation separately documented.

Deep-dive worked examples — Interview answers with actual mechanisms

A. Linux memory incident: free memory low ≠ OOM

Linux page cache unused RAM ko productive use karta hai. free -h mein available estimate dekho, sirf free nahi. Process RSS resident pages count karta hai, lekin shared pages multiple processes mein double-count ho sakte hain; PSS better proportional metric hai. vmstat 1 run queue, swapping, iowait; cat /proc/pressure/memory memory stall pressure; cat /proc/meminfo reclaimable caches; journalctl -k --since '-1 hour' | grep -Ei 'oom|out of memory' kernel OOM events. cgroup v2 workload ke liye memory.current, memory.max, memory.events inspect karo. Agar pod OOMKilled hai to kubectl describe pod and previous logs; memory limit, leak, cache, concurrency, load pattern compare karo. Blind swap add karna fix nahi: swapping latency aur failure mode badal sakti hai.

Interview answer: 'I would first establish whether there is real memory pressure, a cgroup limit breach, or normal page-cache usage. I compare MemAvailable, PSI, OOM logs and per-process PSS. Then I correlate the change with load and deployment, mitigate by reducing concurrency or rolling back, and only resize after capacity analysis.'

B. AWS VPC + ALB + EKS request path

client → recursive DNS → Route53 authoritative response → ALB listener (TLS) → listener rule → target group → pod IP/node target → Kubernetes service routing/application. Exact path depends on ALB target type (ip vs instance) and controller configuration. Verify DNS with dig, TLS with openssl s_client, ALB access logs, target group health, SG/NACL route tables, Kubernetes EndpointSlices and pod readiness. 502 can indicate invalid backend response/connection reset; 504 usually timeout while waiting for target, but use ALB logs and AWS diagnostics rather than guessing. Health check path and app readiness must agree.

C. Terraform S3 state lock recovery

terraform {
  backend "s3" {
    bucket       = "REPLACE-WITH-EXISTING-VERSIONED-STATE-BUCKET"
    key          = "production/network/terraform.tfstate"
    region       = "ap-south-1"
    encrypt      = true
    use_lockfile = true
  }
}

Backend bucket ko pehle create/secure karo; bootstrap and workload states alag rakho. S3 versioning and IAM access restrictions essential. terraform init, terraform validate, terraform plan -out=tfplan run karo. State lock failure par never immediately force-unlock: identify active CI run, state lock ID, team ownership, remote state version, and concurrent deployment. Only verified stale lock ko authorized change ke through terraform force-unlock LOCK_ID karo. S3 native lockfile is supported; DynamoDB locking is deprecated in current Terraform documentation. Never put secrets in backend HCL or commit plan files. Source: https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/backend/s3

D. Kubernetes rollout with security and availability

apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: inference-api
  namespace: ai-prod
spec:
  replicas: 3
  strategy:
    type: RollingUpdate
    rollingUpdate:
      maxUnavailable: 0
      maxSurge: 1
  selector:
    matchLabels: {app: inference-api}
  template:
    metadata:
      labels: {app: inference-api}
    spec:
      securityContext:
        runAsNonRoot: true
        seccompProfile: {type: RuntimeDefault}
      containers:
        - name: api
          image: REPLACE_ECR_URI@sha256:REPLACE_WITH_REAL_DIGEST
          ports: [{containerPort: 8080}]
          resources:
            requests: {cpu: 250m, memory: 256Mi}
            limits: {memory: 512Mi}
          readinessProbe:
            httpGet: {path: /ready, port: 8080}
            periodSeconds: 5
          livenessProbe:
            httpGet: {path: /health, port: 8080}
            periodSeconds: 10
          securityContext:
            allowPrivilegeEscalation: false
            capabilities: {drop: [ALL]}
            readOnlyRootFilesystem: true

YAML is a template: namespace, immutable digest, app routes, writable mounts, service account, topology spread, PDB, NetworkPolicy, autoscaling and actual runtime needs must be configured. kubectl rollout status deploy/inference-api -n ai-prod; kubectl describe pod, kubectl logs --previous, kubectl get endpointslices -n ai-prod; rollback kubectl rollout undo deployment/inference-api -n ai-prod only if previous revision exists and is known good. Probe failures and rollout deadlines must be correlated with startup behavior. Kubernetes docs: https://kubernetes.io/docs/tasks/debug/debug-application/debug-running-pod/

E. Jenkins production release design

Git webhook → Jenkins multibranch checkout → lint/unit tests → SAST/dependency scan → build image → SBOM/image scan → sign image → push ECR → nonprod Helm deployment → integration tests → approval → prod Helm upgrade → rollout health + synthetic tests → audit. Use short-lived AWS credentials through role assumption/federation, scoped permissions, protected branches, immutable digests, and separate prod deploy role. Avoid echoing credentials or passing secrets as command arguments. Jenkins controller should not be used as an unisolated build agent. Rollback requires chart/image revision plus database migration compatibility; helm rollback does not reverse external DB changes.

F. Bedrock vs SageMaker vs GPU EKS

Bedrock: managed model inference APIs; you operate client application, IAM, network access, quotas, observability and spend. SageMaker: model artifact/container/endpoint configuration, deployment, scaling and endpoint health; AWS manages service infrastructure but customer config and model behavior remain your responsibility. GPU EKS: more control over drivers, CUDA, scheduling, serving runtime, batching, observability and cluster security, with greater operational burden. Choice depends on supported model, customization, inference pattern, latency, compliance, scaling, cost and team's expertise.

G. GPU incident — no GPU allocated

Start kubectl describe pod for Insufficient nvidia.com/gpu, taints, affinity and resource requests. On node check nvidia-smi (if driver installed), GPU Operator and device-plugin logs, kubectl describe node allocatable GPUs, runtime class/container runtime compatibility, driver/CUDA support, MIG profile and node labels. Differentiate scheduler cannot place pod from container cannot initialize CUDA and GPU OOM after starting. Do not reinstall drivers blindly on production nodes; cordon/drain with PDB checks and test compatible versions in staging.

H. MLflow + Kubeflow deployment reliability

Kubeflow Pipelines orchestrates pipeline steps and artifacts; MLflow tracks experiments, runs and registry metadata. Production MLflow commonly uses durable SQL backend and object artifact store with proper IAM, encryption, backups and controlled model promotion. Validate pipeline step service accounts, object-store credentials, network egress, image pull, resource quota, metadata persistence and retry semantics. A retrying training step can create duplicate side effects unless idempotent. Model promotion requires evaluation gates, provenance, approval, reproducible image, staged traffic and rollback artifact.

I. Mock interview evaluation rubric

Dimension Points Evidence
Architecture and fundamentals 20 correct data/control path, dependencies
Internal mechanics 20 scheduling, APIs, state, network and storage
Production safety/security 20 blast radius, IAM, rollback, HA
Debugging methodology 20 evidence-first, reproducible diagnosis
Trade-offs and communication 20 alternatives, cost, concise decision

Thresholds: <60 foundational gaps; 60–74 intermediate; 75–84 strong practical; 85+ senior-ready for that topic, subject to live lab verification. A book score does not equal hiring probability.

J. Capstone acceptance checklist

  • Diagram includes users, DNS, TLS, ALB, VPC, EKS/ECS, IAM, model backend, S3, observability.
  • Terraform uses remote locked versioned state, reviewed plans, separate environment boundaries.
  • Jenkins enforces tests, scans, approvals, provenance and reversible deployments.
  • Helm release includes resource limits, probes, non-root, PDB, network policies and rollout gates.
  • Bedrock/SageMaker/GPU inference option justified with latency, throughput and cost measurements.
  • MLflow/Kubeflow model promotion reproducible, auditable and rollback-safe.
  • Multi-AZ failures, restore tests, RTO/RPO, SLO and alerts documented.
  • Red-team IAM privilege escalation paths reviewed; secrets never committed.
  • 3 simulated SEV-1 drills documented with timestamps and actual observations.
  • 90-minute mock interview completed, scored and reattempted after remediation.

Example CIDRs, account IDs, endpoints, regions, resource names, quotas, policies and versions must be adapted. Never execute destructive recovery in production without change approval, backup verification and a rollback plan. The source workbook's 450 framework questions are NOT replaced by 450 fully developed answers in this volume.

Interview mein architecture ke saath incident ka impact, blast radius, rollback aur verification bolna mandatory hai.
