Part I — 45 senior-level technical answers

1. Linux CPU scheduling and processes

Senior interview question: Is technology ka production internal workflow explain karo, kaunse signals se issue isolate karoge, aur design trade-off kya hai?

Model answer — internal mechanism (Hinglish): CFS/EEVDF scheduler tasks ko runnable queues se CPU par dispatch karta hai; scheduling policy aur kernel version behavior change kar sakte hain. User-space shell execve() se process image replace karta hai; fork()/clone() task create karte hain. Context switch mein register state aur memory context transition hota hai.

Evidence and verification: uptime, top -H, pidstat -u -w 1, ps -eo pid,ppid,stat,ni,comm, /proc/<pid>/sched, perf top (permissions required).

Failure diagnosis and decision: High load average ko CPU saturation na samjho: uninterruptible I/O wait bhi load badha sakta hai. Run queue, CPU utilization, steal time, and blocked tasks correlate karo.

Follow-up to practice: Agar primary fix fail ho, blast radius kaise limit karoge, reversible mitigation kya hogi, aur change ke baad user-facing SLI kaise verify karoge? Is answer ko 2-minute whiteboard flow aur 5-minute incident narrative mein practice karo.

2. Linux memory, page cache, OOM

Senior interview question: Is technology ka production internal workflow explain karo, kaunse signals se issue isolate karoge, aur design trade-off kya hai?

Model answer — internal mechanism (Hinglish): Virtual memory page tables se virtual addresses map karti hai; page cache reclaimable hai, anonymous RSS aur swap alag hain. MemAvailable free memory se better pressure signal hai. cgroup v2 memory.max container memory limit enforce karta hai.

Evidence and verification: free -h, vmstat 1, cat /proc/pressure/memory, cat /proc/meminfo, journalctl -k | grep -i oom, cat /sys/fs/cgroup/memory.events.

Failure diagnosis and decision: OOM kill pehle workload/cgroup limit aur OOM logs identify karo; blind swap increase root cause fix nahi hai.

Follow-up to practice: Agar primary fix fail ho, blast radius kaise limit karoge, reversible mitigation kya hogi, aur change ke baad user-facing SLI kaise verify karoge? Is answer ko 2-minute whiteboard flow aur 5-minute incident narrative mein practice karo.

3. Linux networking, DNS and TLS

Senior interview question: Is technology ka production internal workflow explain karo, kaunse signals se issue isolate karoge, aur design trade-off kya hai?

Model answer — internal mechanism (Hinglish): DNS resolver → recursive resolver → authoritative chain; TLS handshake server identity validate karta hai. TCP three-way handshake ke baad HTTP flow hota hai; nftables/conntrack policy path affect karte hain.

Evidence and verification: resolvectl status, dig +trace example.com, ss -tnp, ip route get 1.1.1.1, tcpdump -ni any port 443, openssl s_client -connect example.com:443 -servername example.com.

Failure diagnosis and decision: DNS, TCP, TLS aur HTTP failures ko layers mein isolate karo; curl -v --resolve se DNS vs application difference test karo.

Follow-up to practice: Agar primary fix fail ho, blast radius kaise limit karoge, reversible mitigation kya hogi, aur change ke baad user-facing SLI kaise verify karoge? Is answer ko 2-minute whiteboard flow aur 5-minute incident narrative mein practice karo.

4. Linux storage LVM RAID filesystem

Senior interview question: Is technology ka production internal workflow explain karo, kaunse signals se issue isolate karoge, aur design trade-off kya hai?

Model answer — internal mechanism (Hinglish): LVM PV→VG→LV abstractions provide karta hai; filesystem resize and LV resize separate operations hain. RAID availability improve kar sakta hai, backup replace nahi karta. ext4 shrink offline ho sakta hai; XFS shrink supported nahi.

Evidence and verification: lsblk -f, pvs; vgs; lvs -a -o +devices, cat /proc/mdstat, smartctl -a /dev/<disk>, df -hT, findmnt.

Failure diagnosis and decision: Resize se pehle snapshot/backup, filesystem-specific procedure, free extents, and recovery test verify karo.

Follow-up to practice: Agar primary fix fail ho, blast radius kaise limit karoge, reversible mitigation kya hogi, aur change ke baad user-facing SLI kaise verify karoge? Is answer ko 2-minute whiteboard flow aur 5-minute incident narrative mein practice karo.

5. AWS VPC subnet routing NAT endpoints

Senior interview question: Is technology ka production internal workflow explain karo, kaunse signals se issue isolate karoge, aur design trade-off kya hai?

Model answer — internal mechanism (Hinglish): Subnet route table next hop choose karti hai; public subnet ka matlab IGW route plus public addressing hai. NAT gateway private outbound IPv4 deta hai; interface/gateway endpoints supported AWS services ke private paths de sakte hain.

Evidence and verification: VPC flow logs, Reachability Analyzer, aws ec2 describe-route-tables, aws ec2 describe-security-groups, curl -I from a test instance.

Failure diagnosis and decision: Security group stateful hai; NACL stateless. DNS, return routes, endpoint policy, NAT AZ design check karo.

Follow-up to practice: Agar primary fix fail ho, blast radius kaise limit karoge, reversible mitigation kya hogi, aur change ke baad user-facing SLI kaise verify karoge? Is answer ko 2-minute whiteboard flow aur 5-minute incident narrative mein practice karo.

6. AWS IAM STS workload identity

Senior interview question: Is technology ka production internal workflow explain karo, kaunse signals se issue isolate karoge, aur design trade-off kya hai?

Model answer — internal mechanism (Hinglish): IAM identity policy, resource policy, permissions boundary, SCP, session policy, explicit deny ka evaluation hota hai. STS short-lived credentials issue karta hai; EKS Pod Identity/IRSA pod-to-AWS access enable karte hain.

Evidence and verification: aws sts get-caller-identity, IAM policy simulator, CloudTrail AccessDenied events, aws iam get-role.

Failure diagnosis and decision: Long-lived static keys avoid; trust policy principal/condition aur least privilege resource scope inspect karo.

Follow-up to practice: Agar primary fix fail ho, blast radius kaise limit karoge, reversible mitigation kya hogi, aur change ke baad user-facing SLI kaise verify karoge? Is answer ko 2-minute whiteboard flow aur 5-minute incident narrative mein practice karo.

7. AWS S3 encryption lifecycle replication

Senior interview question: Is technology ka production internal workflow explain karo, kaunse signals se issue isolate karoge, aur design trade-off kya hai?

Model answer — internal mechanism (Hinglish): S3 object storage strongly consistent read-after-write and list operations support karta hai. Bucket policy, KMS key policy, object ownership, Block Public Access independent controls hain. Versioning delete recovery mein help karta hai.

Evidence and verification: aws s3api get-bucket-versioning, get-public-access-block, get-bucket-encryption, CloudTrail data events if enabled.

Failure diagnosis and decision: S3 replication asynchronous; RPO zero assume mat karo. Lifecycle aur Object Lock requirements separately validate karo.

Follow-up to practice: Agar primary fix fail ho, blast radius kaise limit karoge, reversible mitigation kya hogi, aur change ke baad user-facing SLI kaise verify karoge? Is answer ko 2-minute whiteboard flow aur 5-minute incident narrative mein practice karo.

8. Route 53 DNS and health checks

Senior interview question: Is technology ka production internal workflow explain karo, kaunse signals se issue isolate karoge, aur design trade-off kya hai?

Model answer — internal mechanism (Hinglish): Recursive resolvers Route 53 authoritative records query karte hain; TTL caching failover time affect karta hai. Health-check-based routing DNS-level steering hai, existing TCP sessions migrate nahi hote.

Evidence and verification: dig +short app.example.com, dig @<authoritative-ns> app.example.com, Route 53 health-check status and CloudWatch.

Failure diagnosis and decision: DNS propagation/caching aur ALB target health alag layers hain; failover RTO measure karo.

Follow-up to practice: Agar primary fix fail ho, blast radius kaise limit karoge, reversible mitigation kya hogi, aur change ke baad user-facing SLI kaise verify karoge? Is answer ko 2-minute whiteboard flow aur 5-minute incident narrative mein practice karo.

9. ALB NLB and target health

Senior interview question: Is technology ka production internal workflow explain karo, kaunse signals se issue isolate karoge, aur design trade-off kya hai?

Model answer — internal mechanism (Hinglish): ALB layer 7 listener rules HTTP host/path par route karti hain; NLB layer 4 TCP/UDP/TLS flows handle karta hai. ALB targets health checks pass karne par routing eligible hote hain.

Evidence and verification: aws elbv2 describe-target-health, ALB access logs, CloudWatch 5XX/TargetResponseTime, curl -vk.

Failure diagnosis and decision: 502 upstream reset/protocol issue; 504 timeout/network/slowness; 503 no healthy targets/capacity ho sakta hai. Logs se prove karo.

Follow-up to practice: Agar primary fix fail ho, blast radius kaise limit karoge, reversible mitigation kya hogi, aur change ke baad user-facing SLI kaise verify karoge? Is answer ko 2-minute whiteboard flow aur 5-minute incident narrative mein practice karo.

Evidence and verification: kubectl get --raw /readyz, add-on versions, deprecated API scanners, node drain events.

Failure diagnosis and decision: Test workloads, PodDisruptionBudgets, surge capacity, rollback constraints; EKS control plane downgrade generally unavailable.

Follow-up to practice: Agar primary fix fail ho, blast radius kaise limit karoge, reversible mitigation kya hogi, aur change ke baad user-facing SLI kaise verify karoge? Is answer ko 2-minute whiteboard flow aur 5-minute incident narrative mein practice karo.

44. ML pipeline promotion rollback

Senior interview question: Is technology ka production internal workflow explain karo, kaunse signals se issue isolate karoge, aur design trade-off kya hai?

Model answer — internal mechanism (Hinglish): Model promotion needs artifact provenance, evaluation thresholds, model registry stage/alias, canary traffic and monitoring for drift.

Evidence and verification: Evaluation metrics, model signature, request/response error rates, canary performance and rollback events.

Failure diagnosis and decision: Model rollback and feature/schema rollback coupled; shadow testing before broad traffic.

Follow-up to practice: Agar primary fix fail ho, blast radius kaise limit karoge, reversible mitigation kya hogi, aur change ke baad user-facing SLI kaise verify karoge? Is answer ko 2-minute whiteboard flow aur 5-minute incident narrative mein practice karo.

45. Enterprise AI platform system design

Senior interview question: Is technology ka production internal workflow explain karo, kaunse signals se issue isolate karoge, aur design trade-off kya hai?

Model answer — internal mechanism (Hinglish): Enterprise AI platform has edge identity, app tier, model inference, data stores, CI/CD, observability, compliance and DR planes. Bedrock managed API vs SageMaker managed hosting vs EKS self-hosted GPU serve different control/cost needs.

Evidence and verification: Architecture diagram, threat model, load-test reports, IaC plan, SLO dashboard and DR exercise.

Failure diagnosis and decision: Define nonfunctional requirements and capacity/cost estimates before choosing services.

Follow-up to practice: Agar primary fix fail ho, blast radius kaise limit karoge, reversible mitigation kya hogi, aur change ke baad user-facing SLI kaise verify karoge? Is answer ko 2-minute whiteboard flow aur 5-minute incident narrative mein practice karo.

Part II — Hands-on production labs

Lab A — Terraform: multi-AZ VPC foundation and secure remote state

Goal: Public ALB subnets + private workload subnets in two AZs. State backend bootstrap separately; never create backend bucket in the same config before terraform init. This sample is a sandbox skeleton; production requires endpoints, flow logs, NAT egress, route policy, security groups, logging and governance.

Where: Debian 12 workstation with Terraform and AWS CLI installed; use sandbox credentials (aws sts get-caller-identity).

infra/main.tf:

terraform {
  required_version = ">= 1.6.0"
  required_providers {
    aws = { source = "hashicorp/aws", version = "~> 5.0" }
  }
}
provider "aws" { region = var.region }
variable "region" { type = string; default = "ap-south-1" }
variable "environment" { type = string; default = "sandbox" }
data "aws_availability_zones" "available" { state = "available" }
resource "aws_vpc" "main" {
  cidr_block = "10.42.0.0/16"
  enable_dns_hostnames = true
  enable_dns_support = true
  tags = { Name = "${var.environment}-vpc" }
}
resource "aws_internet_gateway" "main" { vpc_id = aws_vpc.main.id }
resource "aws_subnet" "public" {
  count = 2
  vpc_id = aws_vpc.main.id
  cidr_block = cidrsubnet(aws_vpc.main.cidr_block, 8, count.index)
  availability_zone = data.aws_availability_zones.available.names[count.index]
  map_public_ip_on_launch = false
  tags = { Name = "public-${count.index}", Tier = "public" }
}
resource "aws_subnet" "private" {
  count = 2
  vpc_id = aws_vpc.main.id
  cidr_block = cidrsubnet(aws_vpc.main.cidr_block, 8, count.index + 10)
  availability_zone = data.aws_availability_zones.available.names[count.index]
  tags = { Name = "private-${count.index}", Tier = "private" }
}
resource "aws_route_table" "public" { vpc_id = aws_vpc.main.id }
resource "aws_route" "internet" {
  route_table_id = aws_route_table.public.id
  destination_cidr_block = "0.0.0.0/0"
  gateway_id = aws_internet_gateway.main.id
}
resource "aws_route_table_association" "public" {
  count = 2
  subnet_id = aws_subnet.public[count.index].id
  route_table_id = aws_route_table.public.id
}
output "vpc_id" { value = aws_vpc.main.id }
output "private_subnet_ids" { value = aws_subnet.private[*].id }

Explain: count creates two subnets; cidrsubnet(...,8,index) yields /24s; data.aws_availability_zones chooses current available AZ names; private subnets have no default route here, so no outbound internet. Public route exists but subnet instances have no automatic public IP. This is deliberate deny-by-default. Add endpoints/NAT according to application needs.

State bootstrap (separate administrator-managed bucket): Configure S3 bucket with versioning, encryption, Block Public Access, access logging and least-privilege policies. Terraform S3 backend use_lockfile=true is supported by current Terraform; confirm installed version and backend compatibility. Never copy live state into source control.

infra/backend.tf.example:

terraform {
  backend "s3" {
    bucket       = "REPLACE-UNIQUE-STATE-BUCKET"
    key          = "sandbox/network/terraform.tfstate"
    region       = "ap-south-1"
    encrypt      = true
    use_lockfile = true
  }
}

Execute: terraform fmt -recursive; terraform init (after backend setup); terraform validate; terraform plan -out=tfplan; review plan; terraform apply tfplan only with approval. Verify: aws ec2 describe-vpcs --vpc-ids "$(terraform output -raw vpc_id)"; inspect route tables and subnet AZs. Rollback: approved terraform destroy only in disposable sandbox after confirming no dependent resources. Failure drill: AccessDenied on backend? Check IAM S3 object and .tflock permissions, bucket policy and KMS key policy; never force-unlock a live operator's lock.

Lab B — Ansible-managed Nginx reverse proxy

Goal: Configure a Debian 12 Nginx edge with idempotent playbook, validated configuration, safe reload, and limited SSH privileges. Backend 127.0.0.1:8000 must exist. TLS termination belongs at ALB or separately configured certificate endpoint; this example is HTTP-only for isolated lab.

inventory.ini:

[proxy]
proxy01 ansible_host=192.0.2.10 ansible_user=deployer

site.yml:

---
- name: Configure nginx reverse proxy
  hosts: proxy
  become: true
  gather_facts: true
  tasks:
    - name: Install nginx
      ansible.builtin.apt:
        name: nginx
        state: present
        update_cache: true
    - name: Deploy reverse proxy config
      ansible.builtin.template:
        src: templates/app.conf.j2
        dest: /etc/nginx/sites-available/app.conf
        owner: root
        group: root
        mode: '0644'
      notify: Validate and reload nginx
    - name: Enable site
      ansible.builtin.file:
        src: /etc/nginx/sites-available/app.conf
        dest: /etc/nginx/sites-enabled/app.conf
        state: link
      notify: Validate and reload nginx
    - name: Disable default site
      ansible.builtin.file:
        path: /etc/nginx/sites-enabled/default
        state: absent
      notify: Validate and reload nginx
    - name: Ensure nginx is started
      ansible.builtin.service:
        name: nginx
        state: started
        enabled: true
  handlers:
    - name: Validate and reload nginx
      ansible.builtin.shell: nginx -t && systemctl reload nginx
      changed_when: true

templates/app.conf.j2:

server {
    listen 80;
    server_name _;
    access_log /var/log/nginx/app_access.log;
    error_log /var/log/nginx/app_error.log warn;
    location /healthz { proxy_pass http://127.0.0.1:8000/healthz; }
    location / {
        proxy_set_header Host $host;
        proxy_set_header X-Real-IP $remote_addr;
        proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-For $proxy_add_x_forwarded_for;
        proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-Proto $scheme;
        proxy_connect_timeout 5s;
        proxy_read_timeout 30s;
        proxy_pass http://127.0.0.1:8000;
    }
}

Run: ansible-playbook -i inventory.ini site.yml --syntax-check; ansible-playbook -i inventory.ini site.yml --check --diff (check mode not always perfect for new installs); ansible-playbook -i inventory.ini site.yml; curl -I http://192.0.2.10/healthz from authorized lab network. 192.0.2.10 is documentation-only, replace it. Production correction: handler nginx -t && reload validates after writing; a bad template remains on disk if validation fails. Prefer a staging-and-validation deployment strategy or Ansible validate on the template task (validate: '/usr/sbin/nginx -t -c %s' only if the staged file is a complete nginx.conf); keep previous known-good config for rollback.

Lab C — Kubernetes: rollout safety, RBAC, probes, incident evidence

k8s/app.yaml:

apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: api
  namespace: demo
spec:
  replicas: 3
  strategy:
    type: RollingUpdate
    rollingUpdate:
      maxUnavailable: 0
      maxSurge: 1
  selector:
    matchLabels: { app: api }
  template:
    metadata:
      labels: { app: api }
    spec:
      automountServiceAccountToken: false
      securityContext:
        runAsNonRoot: true
        seccompProfile: { type: RuntimeDefault }
      containers:
        - name: api
          image: nginxinc/nginx-unprivileged:stable
          ports: [{ containerPort: 8080 }]
          securityContext:
            allowPrivilegeEscalation: false
            capabilities: { drop: ["ALL"] }
            readOnlyRootFilesystem: true
          resources:
            requests: { cpu: 100m, memory: 64Mi }
            limits: { memory: 256Mi }
          readinessProbe:
            httpGet: { path: /, port: 8080 }
            periodSeconds: 5
          livenessProbe:
            httpGet: { path: /, port: 8080 }
            periodSeconds: 15
          volumeMounts:
            - { name: cache, mountPath: /tmp }
            - { name: run, mountPath: /var/run }
      volumes:
        - { name: cache, emptyDir: {} }
        - { name: run, emptyDir: {} }
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: Service
metadata: { name: api, namespace: demo }
spec:
  selector: { app: api }
  ports: [{ port: 80, targetPort: 8080 }]
---
apiVersion: policy/v1
kind: PodDisruptionBudget
metadata: { name: api, namespace: demo }
spec:
  minAvailable: 2
  selector: { matchLabels: { app: api } }

Why: maxUnavailable: 0 requests no voluntary rollout capacity loss; actual zero downtime still depends on enough schedulable capacity, readiness accuracy, application shutdown, and external routing. automountServiceAccountToken:false reduces token exposure. Read-only root filesystem requires writable /tmp and runtime paths; verify image compatibility. Image tag stable is illustrative: pin immutable digest in production.

Commands: kubectl create ns demo; kubectl apply --dry-run=client -f k8s/app.yaml; kubectl apply -f k8s/app.yaml; kubectl -n demo rollout status deploy/api --timeout=180s; kubectl -n demo get pods -o wide; kubectl -n demo get endpointslice; kubectl -n demo describe pod <pod>; kubectl -n demo logs <pod> --previous. Rollback: kubectl -n demo rollout undo deployment/api only after confirming previous ReplicaSet and external state compatibility. Troubleshoot: Pending→capacity/taints; CrashLoop→logs/probes; no endpoints→readiness/selector; ImagePullBackOff→registry auth/image digest.

Lab D — Jenkins pipeline with Helm release gates

Jenkinsfile:

pipeline {
  agent { label 'trusted-k8s-deployer' }
  options { timestamps(); timeout(time: 30, unit: 'MINUTES'); disableConcurrentBuilds() }
  environment { NAMESPACE = 'demo'; RELEASE = 'api' }
  stages {
    stage('Validate chart') {
      steps {
        sh 'helm lint ./chart && helm template "$RELEASE" ./chart --namespace "$NAMESPACE" > rendered.yaml'
        sh 'kubectl apply --dry-run=client -f rendered.yaml'
      }
    }
    stage('Review production deployment') {
      steps { input message: 'Approve release to target cluster?' }
    }
    stage('Deploy') {
      steps {
        sh 'helm upgrade --install "$RELEASE" ./chart --namespace "$NAMESPACE" --create-namespace --atomic --wait --timeout 10m'
        sh 'kubectl -n "$NAMESPACE" rollout status deployment/api --timeout=180s'
      }
    }
  }
  post { always { archiveArtifacts artifacts: 'rendered.yaml', allowEmptyArchive: true } }
}

Important: This is a deployment-stage example, not a full secure supply-chain pipeline. Production requires isolated agents, OIDC/short-lived AWS credentials, build/test/scan/sign/push of immutable images, admission policies, trusted branch protections, environment-specific values, and smoke/rollback testing. --atomic handles supported Helm release rollback but not irreversible DB migrations. Failure drill: chart lint passes but pod fails readiness: kubectl describe pod, kubectl logs --previous, helm history, compare image digest and Secret/ConfigMap references.

Lab E — Boto3 Bedrock client with bounded retries and observability

bedrock_client.py:

import json
import logging
import os
import time
import boto3
from botocore.config import Config
from botocore.exceptions import ClientError, BotoCoreError

logging.basicConfig(level=logging.INFO)
log = logging.getLogger('inference')
REGION = os.environ.get('AWS_REGION', 'ap-south-1')
MODEL_ID = os.environ['BEDROCK_MODEL_ID']  # choose a supported model/inference profile
client = boto3.client('bedrock-runtime', region_name=REGION,
    config=Config(connect_timeout=5, read_timeout=60,
                  retries={'mode': 'standard', 'max_attempts': 4}))

def invoke(prompt: str) -> str:
    start = time.monotonic()
    try:
        response = client.converse(
            modelId=MODEL_ID,
            messages=[{'role': 'user', 'content': [{'text': prompt}]}],
            inferenceConfig={'maxTokens': 256, 'temperature': 0.2})
        blocks = response['output']['message']['content']
        return ''.join(b.get('text', '') for b in blocks)
    except ClientError as exc:
        code = exc.response.get('Error', {}).get('Code', 'Unknown')
        request_id = exc.response.get('ResponseMetadata', {}).get('RequestId')
        log.error('bedrock_error code=%s request_id=%s', code, request_id)
        raise
    except BotoCoreError:
        log.exception('bedrock_transport_error')
        raise
    finally:
        log.info('inference_latency_ms=%.1f', (time.monotonic()-start)*1000)

if __name__ == '__main__':
    print(invoke('Explain readiness vs liveness probes in one paragraph.'))

Execute: Python venv → pip install boto3 → export AWS_REGION=... BEDROCK_MODEL_ID=... → aws sts get-caller-identity → python bedrock_client.py. Model must support Converse API and be available to your account/region; IAM must permit bedrock:InvokeModel on appropriate resources. Never log user prompts or secrets by default. Retry mode helps throttling/transient errors but cannot fix missing model access, unsupported region, or invalid input. Monitor cost, tokens, latency and rate limits.

Lab F — NVIDIA GPU, MLflow, Kubeflow, SageMaker operational runbooks

GPU node investigation: kubectl describe node <gpu-node> → inspect Capacity/Allocatable nvidia.com/gpu → kubectl describe pod <gpu-pod> → device plugin/GPU Operator pods → on authorized node nvidia-smi, dmesg and driver module status. nvidia.com/gpu scheduling requires compatible drivers, runtime, device plugin and node resource advertisement. Do not reinstall GPU drivers on production nodes before cordon/drain and capacity assessment.

Minimal GPU scheduling manifest:

apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata: { name: gpu-smoke, namespace: demo }
spec:
  restartPolicy: Never
  containers:
    - name: check
      image: nvidia/cuda:12.4.1-base-ubuntu22.04
      command: ["nvidia-smi"]
      resources:
        limits:
          nvidia.com/gpu: 1

Pin image digest and confirm CUDA compatibility before real use. kubectl -n demo logs gpu-smoke; a Pending pod may indicate insufficient GPUs, selectors/taints, or quota. If GPU resource is absent from node allocatable, investigate plugin/driver before modifying scheduling.

MLflow operational contract: PostgreSQL-backed tracking DB + S3 artifact store + authenticated/authorized MLflow service. Verify DB migrations/connection pooling, S3 permissions, server health, backups and restores. MLflow server without proper authentication must never be exposed publicly. Production launch commands and options vary by MLflow version: pin version and verify current CLI documentation before deployment.

Kubeflow operational contract: pipeline execution needs namespace-scoped service account, container registry pull, artifact object storage, controller permissions and adequate CPU/GPU quota. Diagnose Pending via scheduling events; ImagePullBackOff via image credentials; AccessDenied via pod identity; OOMKilled via container memory limit and usage; failed artifact upload via S3 policy/endpoint. Pipeline code must pin component images and define retries/idempotency.

SageMaker endpoint incident: aws sagemaker describe-endpoint --endpoint-name <name>; check EndpointStatus and FailureReason; inspect CloudWatch model container logs; confirm image URI, model artifact S3 path, execution role, VPC config, instance quota, health-check timeouts, and inference request contract. Deployment changes should use staged endpoint configuration and canary/rollback plan. Avoid logging sensitive inference inputs.

Part III — Production incident response framework

Example: ALB 504 during deployment

Impact: 504 spike, elevated p99, user requests timing out. First 5 minutes: Declare severity based on SLO; freeze concurrent changes; correlate deployment timestamp, ALB HTTPCode_ELB_5XX_Count, TargetResponseTime, target health and app error rate. Hypotheses: app slow/deadlocked, upstream network, overloaded backend, connection pool exhaustion, timeout mismatch. Safe mitigation: if correlated with new rollout, revert image/config with deployment controller while preserving logs and checking database migration compatibility. Evidence: ALB access log target_processing_time, application traces, pod restarts, readiness, CPU/memory and RDS connections. Verification: error rate and p99 recover, synthetic transaction passes, backlog drains. Prevention: staged rollout, SLO gate, load test and timeout budget review.

Example: Terraform state lock conflict

Impact: deploy pipeline blocked, no runtime impact by default. First step: Check whether another plan/apply is active and identify lock owner. Never remove live lock. If stale: document lock ID, inspect backend S3 lock object and CI run, confirm no active operation, take state backup and follow approved terraform force-unlock process. Verify: terraform plan reconciles against real infrastructure; investigate drift before apply. Prevention: serialized CI environment locks, least-privilege state IAM, versioned state bucket.

Example: GPU workloads pending

Impact: model throughput zero/low. Evidence: Pod FailedScheduling, node allocatable GPU, device plugin logs, taints, tolerations, quotas, autoscaler logs. Mitigation: route to healthy model-serving replicas or managed fallback if architecture supports; do not force-delete unrelated workloads. Root causes: GPU unavailable, wrong node selector, GPU operator/driver issue, EC2 GPU capacity/quota. Verify: pod scheduled, nvidia-smi works, inference smoke test passes, latency and GPU metrics recover.

Part IV — Interview readiness scoring

For each of 45 topics, score 0–4 on concept, internal mechanism, architecture, debugging, security, and trade-offs (24 max/topic). 0=unknown, 1=definition only, 2=explains flow, 3=works through evidence, 4=production-safe and explains alternatives. Senior interview practice threshold: at least 18/24 in each of the 12 core topics, plus 3 timed whiteboard designs and 3 timed incident simulations. This is a suggested training rubric, not an industry-standard hiring threshold.

Core topics: Linux memory/process/network/storage; VPC/IAM; EKS networking/security; Terraform; Jenkins; ALB; observability. Specialized MLOps roles additionally require Bedrock/SageMaker, GPU, MLflow/Kubeflow and model-serving depth.

Chapter 01 — Lab baseline, architecture aur diagnostic discipline

Learning outcome

Is chapter ke baad aap control-plane vs data-plane, desired-state reconciliation, aur evidence-first incident response ko interview mein defend kar sakte hain.

Kubernetes ek distributed control system hai: API Server authoritative interface hai, etcd durable desired/observed object data store karta hai, controllers continuously observed state ko desired state ki taraf reconcile karte hain, scheduler unscheduled Pods ke liye node select karta hai, aur kubelet node par Pod lifecycle ko container runtime ke through maintain karta hai. Running ka matlab application healthy hona nahi: readiness, endpoint membership, process listen port, aur actual request path alag checks hain.

Operator -> kubectl -> HTTPS -> kube-apiserver -> etcd
                                 ^          |
                     watch/list  |          | stored objects
                                 |
     controllers / scheduler / kubelets
                   |                  \
             bind/update           CRI runtime -> CNI setup -> pod

Debian 12 lab environment

Where: admin workstation (Debian 12), with kubectl installed and kubeconfig for a disposable cluster. Avoid production experiments. For kind lab, install a supported container runtime and kind from official release artifacts; pin versions, verify checksums, and record versions rather than blindly curl-to-shell. EKS exercises require a separately configured AWS account and may incur charges.

kubectl version --client
kubectl config get-contexts
kubectl config current-context
kubectl cluster-info
kubectl get nodes -o wide
kubectl get --raw='/readyz?verbose'
kubectl get --raw='/livez?verbose'
kubectl api-resources | head -30
kubectl get --raw='/version'

kubectl version --client checks local binary, config current-context identifies target cluster, cluster-info verifies API endpoint reachability, /readyz is API-server readiness, api-resources uses discovery. Never assume the current context is a test cluster. Production change checklist: change ticket, cluster/account ID, blast radius, pre-change backup, rollback, owner, maintenance window.

Baseline namespace and workload

apiVersion: v1
kind: Namespace
metadata:
  name: netlab
  labels:
    purpose: training
---
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: echo
  namespace: netlab
spec:
  replicas: 2
  selector:
    matchLabels: {app: echo}
  template:
    metadata:
      labels: {app: echo}
    spec:
      containers:
      - name: echo
        image: registry.k8s.io/e2e-test-images/agnhost:2.45
        args: ["netexec", "--http-port=8080"]
        ports: [{containerPort: 8080}]
        readinessProbe:
          httpGet: {path: /, port: 8080}
          initialDelaySeconds: 2
          periodSeconds: 5
        resources:
          requests: {cpu: 50m, memory: 64Mi}
          limits: {memory: 128Mi}
---

kind: Service
metadata:
  name: echo
  namespace: netlab

  selector: {app: echo}
  ports: [{port: 80, targetPort: 8080}]

Apply from admin workstation: kubectl apply -f netlab.yaml; verify with kubectl -n netlab rollout status deployment/echo, get pods -o wide, get endpointslices -l kubernetes.io/service-name=echo. Image availability and probe behavior should be validated for the pinned version in your environment. Remove with kubectl delete -f netlab.yaml after labs.

Interview drill

Q: If etcd stores objects, why not edit etcd directly? A: Bypasses Kubernetes API admission, validation, concurrency and consistency guarantees; may corrupt cluster state. Follow-up: What happens if API server is unavailable? Existing running containers generally continue, but new changes, scheduling and many controllers cannot progress normally.

Chapter 02 — kubectl request ka complete lifecycle

Client side: kubeconfig, discovery, request formation

kubectl is a client, not a control-plane daemon. It loads kubeconfig context (cluster endpoint, CA, user authentication method, namespace), resolves resource kinds via discovery/OpenAPI, serializes YAML to API JSON, and sends HTTPS requests. Authentication might use client certificates, bearer tokens, or an exec credential plugin (for example EKS AWS CLI). TLS authenticates server; user credentials authenticate client. Authentication ≠ authorization.

kubectl config view --minify --raw=false
kubectl -v=8 get pods -n netlab 2>kubectl-debug.log
kubectl auth can-i create deployments -n netlab
kubectl explain deployment.spec.strategy
kubectl get --raw /apis/apps/v1 | head -c 400

-v=8 prints request URLs and timing; logs can contain sensitive metadata and should be sanitized. auth can-i tests effective RBAC for current identity; explain consults API schema. Avoid --raw kubeconfig output in tickets.

API server pipeline (simplified but precise)

  1. HTTPS listener receives request, handles authentication (identity), authorization (RBAC/other configured authorizers), mutating admission (where configured), API object defaulting and validation, validating admission and storage strategy. Exact internal ordering and admission behavior vary by API operation/version; do not memorize one universal hook order.
  2. On create/update, API server persists validated state in etcd with concurrency/version semantics (metadata.resourceVersion). On server-side apply, field ownership (managedFields) helps prevent competing managers overwriting fields silently.
  3. Watches propagate object changes to Deployment controller; controller creates/updates ReplicaSet, which creates Pod objects. Scheduler watches pending Pods without node assignment, filters/scores nodes, binds selected node. Kubelet watches Pods assigned to its node, prepares volumes/secrets, invokes CRI runtime to create sandbox/containers and invokes configured networking integration. Kubelet updates Pod status back via API server.
  4. Service endpoint controller / EndpointSlice controller tracks eligible endpoints; networking implementation programs packet forwarding.

Critical correction: kubectl apply returning configured is not evidence of successful rollout. Use rollout status, readiness and end-to-end requests.

Apply modes and failure analysis

kubectl diff -f netlab.yaml
kubectl apply --server-side --field-manager=platform-team -f netlab.yaml
kubectl -n netlab rollout status deployment/echo --timeout=120s
kubectl -n netlab get deploy echo -o yaml | sed -n '1,100p'
kubectl -n netlab get events --sort-by=.lastTimestamp | tail -20

Client-side apply historically uses last-applied annotation; server-side apply tracks field managers and conflicts. Do not use --force-conflicts reflexively in production. kubectl get is GET/LIST; kubectl logs asks API server to proxy a kubelet log request; kubectl exec opens a streaming connection via API server/kubelet to runtime; port-forward forwards a local TCP stream and is not a substitute for production ingress.

Diagnose 401/403/timeouts

  • Unauthorized (401): credentials expired, malformed, wrong authentication integration; check identity and token refresh.
  • Forbidden (403): authenticated but lacks verb/resource/namespace permissions; kubectl auth can-i and RoleBindings.
  • TLS x509: CA, hostname or certificate issue; do not bypass with insecure-skip-tls-verify.
  • context deadline exceeded: DNS, endpoint routing, SG/firewall, API overload or network path; use kubectl -v=8, DNS resolution, TLS handshake checks and control-plane metrics.
  • the server could not find the requested resource: wrong group/version, missing CRD, stale discovery.

Interview

Q: What exactly does kubectl apply -f deployment.yaml do? A: It submits desired object fields through the API; controllers asynchronously create/replace Pods. It does not directly contact worker nodes, schedule containers or allocate IPs. Follow-up: How to prove deployment healthy? A: desired vs ready replicas, events, EndpointSlices, probes, logs, service request, latency/error SLO.

Chapter 03 — Control plane internals: etcd, scheduler, controllers, kubelet, CRI

etcd and watch

etcd is a strongly consistent distributed key-value store based on Raft. API server reads/writes through its storage layer; controllers normally watch API objects rather than contacting etcd directly. Raft quorum is needed for writes. In a three-member cluster, losing two voting members prevents progress on writes. Snapshots must be protected and restoration rehearsed; snapshots contain secrets and sensitive cluster state.

kubectl -n kube-system get pods -o wide
kubectl get --raw='/metrics' | head -n 5
kubectl get pod -A --field-selector=status.phase=Pending
kubectl describe pod -n netlab <pod-name>

Where: admin workstation. Metrics endpoint access may be restricted; large /metrics output should not be copied into tickets. For managed EKS control planes, you cannot SSH into AWS-managed API servers or etcd. Use supported control-plane logs and metrics instead.

Scheduler decision

Scheduling consists of queueing, filtering infeasible nodes (resource requests, taints/tolerations, affinity, topology, volume constraints), scoring feasible nodes, then binding. Scheduler uses requests for resource accounting, not observed CPU usage. A Pending Pod can result from insufficient allocatable CPU/memory, topology constraints, PVC binding, node selectors, or taints.

kubectl describe pod -n netlab <pending-pod>
kubectl get nodes -o custom-columns=NAME:.metadata.name,ALLOC_CPU:.status.allocatable.cpu,ALLOC_MEM:.status.allocatable.memory
kubectl describe node <node-name>

Kubelet and runtime

Kubelet supervises desired Pods assigned to its node. It asks CRI-compatible runtime (typically containerd or CRI-O) to create Pod sandbox and containers. CNI is normally invoked by runtime networking integration to attach pod sandbox networking; exact mechanism varies. Kubelet performs liveness/readiness/startup probes, reports status, manages mounted volumes via CSI coordination, and handles termination grace periods.

Where: worker-node shell (only if authorized):

sudo systemctl status kubelet
sudo journalctl -u kubelet -n 100 --no-pager
sudo crictl info
sudo crictl pods
sudo crictl ps -a

On EKS managed nodes, node OS/image and access method vary; Fargate does not provide normal node shell access.

Incident: Pods Pending after node scaling

Observe FailedScheduling, check taints/affinity, PVC topology, node allocatable and cluster autoscaler/Karpenter logs. Do not blindly scale node count before determining constraints; a PVC AZ mismatch cannot be fixed by arbitrary extra nodes.

Interview

Q: Who creates the Pod? A: Deployment controller creates ReplicaSet; ReplicaSet controller creates Pod API objects; scheduler assigns node; kubelet/runtime materialize containers. Q: Does kube-proxy schedule pods? A: No, it implements Service forwarding in relevant modes.

Chapter 04 — Linux network namespace, veth, pod sandbox aur same-node packet flow

Core mental model

Each normal Pod has a shared network namespace across its containers: same IP, port space and loopback. Multiple containers in one Pod can communicate over localhost, but two different Pods cannot treat 127.0.0.1 as each other. Linux network namespaces isolate interfaces, routes, ARP/neighbor tables and sockets. A veth pair is a virtual Ethernet cable: one end inside pod namespace, other in host namespace or connected to a bridge/datapath. Some CNIs (notably AWS VPC CNI) use different plumbing; do not assume every Pod has cni0 bridge.

Pod A netns                Linux node                 Pod B netns
 app -> eth0 -- vethA <-> bridge/eBPF/routes <-> vethB -- eth0 -> app
 10.244.1.10                                             10.244.1.11

Packet path: application calls connect()/sendto(); kernel builds TCP/UDP/IP packet, checks pod route, emits via pod interface; peer end enters host datapath; forwarding decision (bridge FDB, route, policy/eBPF) sends toward destination veth; destination kernel delivers to listening socket. No Service VIP or DNS required for direct Pod-IP communication.

Observe safely in a disposable node

Where: node shell. These commands inspect; no network mutation.

ip -br link
ip route
ip rule
sudo lsns -t net
sudo ss -lntup | head -30
sudo tcpdump -ni any 'tcp port 8080' -c 20

Find Pod sandbox PID with crictl inspectp <sandbox-id> (field paths depend on runtime); inspect namespace with sudo nsenter -t <pid> -n ip addr and ip route. Avoid assuming kubectl exec images include ip or tcpdump. For ephemeral debug containers, use approved images, RBAC and Pod Security settings.

MTU, ARP, conntrack

Overlay VXLAN/Geneve adds encapsulation overhead and reduces effective payload MTU. PMTU discovery blocked by ICMP filtering can cause small requests to work while large requests hang. ARP/neighbor resolution applies to local L2 hops; routed or eBPF setups differ. Conntrack tracks stateful flows and NAT mappings; table exhaustion can drop new connections. Inspect nstat, ip -s link, ss -s, conntrack -S (if installed), and kernel logs.

Incident

Symptom: Pod A can ping Pod B but TCP port 8080 fails. Check: is destination process listening on 0.0.0.0:8080 vs 127.0.0.1? ss -lntp; NetworkPolicy TCP rules; container port is metadata, not an open listener. Ping may be disallowed independently of TCP.

Interview

Q: Do containers in the same Pod need a Service to talk? A: No, shared network namespace means localhost works. Q: Can Pod IP change? A: Yes on recreation; use Service/DNS for stable discovery.

Chapter 05 — Cross-node networking, overlays, routing, CNI and AWS VPC CNI

Cross-node packet journey

Pod A on Node 1 sends to Pod B IP on Node 2. Source node routes based on CNI data plane; an overlay may encapsulate original Pod packet in VXLAN/Geneve and send node-to-node; routed/BGP designs route Pod CIDRs natively; AWS VPC CNI normally assigns VPC-routable pod IPs to ENI-managed interfaces. Destination node decapsulates if necessary, applies policy and forwards into Pod namespace. Same-node and cross-node paths can be different.

Pod A (Node 1) -> host route/CNI -> underlay VPC/L3 -> Node 2 CNI -> Pod B
                       | overlay encapsulation OR native routing |

CNI is a specification/interface for adding/removing container network connectivity, not a single daemon. Implementations vary: Calico can use routed, BGP or encapsulated networking and policy; Cilium commonly uses eBPF datapath and policy; AWS VPC CNI manages pod IP assignment through ENIs/IPAMD, with prefix delegation and custom networking options. Plugin and mode matter when tracing packets.

EKS VPC CNI operations

  • IP availability depends on subnet free addresses, instance ENI/IP capacity, CNI settings and chosen prefix-delegation mode.
  • Worker security groups, subnet route tables, NACLs and node-to-node reachability matter. AWS network policy features depend on add-on/version/configuration.
  • For prefix delegation, contiguous free prefix capacity can matter; fragmentation can prevent prefix allocation despite free scattered addresses.
  • On Fargate, network implementation and debugging differ from EC2 worker nodes.
kubectl -n kube-system get daemonset aws-node -o wide
kubectl -n kube-system get pods -l k8s-app=aws-node -o wide
kubectl -n kube-system logs daemonset/aws-node --all-containers --tail=100
kubectl get pods -A -o wide
kubectl describe node <node>

AWS CLI (authorized workstation):

aws eks describe-cluster --name "$CLUSTER" --region "$AWS_REGION" --query 'cluster.resourcesVpcConfig'
aws ec2 describe-subnets --subnet-ids "$SUBNET_ID" --region "$AWS_REGION" --query 'Subnets[*].[SubnetId,AvailableIpAddressCount]'

CNI failure incident

FailedCreatePodSandBox with IP allocation failure: check aws-node logs, ENI capacity, free subnet IPs, permissions for CNI role, API connectivity, and add-on health. Do not assume it is an application bug. If pod IP is assigned but remote traffic fails, compare routes/SG/NACL and capture packet at both nodes where feasible.

Interview

Q: Why might 100 free subnet IPs still not allow new Pods? A: Instance ENI/IP limits, prefix allocation fragmentation, warm IP/prefix targets, CNI errors or quota can constrain placement. Q: Does Kubernetes itself define one pod overlay? A: No; networking implementation is provided by CNI and underlying network.

Chapter 06 — Services, EndpointSlices, kube-proxy, NAT and conntrack

Why Service exists

Pod IPs are ephemeral; a Service gives stable virtual IP and DNS name for a selected set of ready endpoints. Service controller allocates ClusterIP, EndpointSlice controller publishes endpoint data, and dataplane implementation forwards connections. port is Service-facing port; targetPort is Pod destination port. Selector mismatch gives empty EndpointSlices even if Pods are Running.

client pod -> DNS resolves echo.netlab.svc.cluster.local
          -> ClusterIP:80 -> Service dataplane -> ready PodIP:8080

ClusterIP internal virtual IP. NodePort exposes port on nodes (subject to dataplane and network restrictions). LoadBalancer requests external/internal LB integration from cloud controller or LB implementation. ExternalName DNS CNAME, not proxy. Headless Service (clusterIP: None) supports direct endpoint discovery and StatefulSet patterns.

kube-proxy modes

iptables mode installs netfilter chains and DNAT rules for service VIPs. IPVS mode uses kernel IPVS virtual servers (availability/support depend on Kubernetes distribution/version); eBPF service implementations can replace kube-proxy behavior in certain CNIs. Kubernetes has been moving toward nftables support on supported platforms. Never assume iptables -t nat shows all forwarding rules on an eBPF-based cluster. Existing TCP connections often remain pinned to a backend through conntrack even after endpoints change; new connections can choose new backends.

kubectl -n netlab get svc echo -o wide
kubectl -n netlab get endpointslices -l kubernetes.io/service-name=echo -o yaml
kubectl -n netlab describe svc echo
kubectl -n kube-system get ds kube-proxy
# Node shell only; iptables-mode example:
sudo iptables-save -t nat | grep -E 'KUBE-SVC|KUBE-SEP' | head
sudo conntrack -L -p tcp 2>/dev/null | head

kubectl get endpoints legacy object may not fully represent large/modern endpoint data; prefer EndpointSlices. kube-proxy may not exist in replacement dataplanes.

Hands-on validation

Run an approved debug pod with curl/dig, request http://echo.netlab.svc.cluster.local, compare kubectl get endpointslices with Ready Pod IPs. Scale Deployment from 2 to 0: Service IP may remain, but there are no ready backends. Expected: connections fail; exact HTTP/TCP error depends on dataplane.

Incident: 503 after rollout

Check kubectl get pods -l app=echo, describe readiness, get endpointslices, ingress target registration, Service targetPort, and rollout revision. A Service with zero endpoints is not repaired by changing DNS.

Interview

Q: Does CoreDNS load-balance every HTTP request? A: Usually CoreDNS resolves the Service name to ClusterIP; Service dataplane selects backend per connection/flow. Headless service DNS behavior differs.

Chapter 07 — CoreDNS: complete resolution path and DNS failures

DNS path

Application resolver uses Pod /etc/resolv.conf, whose nameserver commonly points to cluster DNS Service IP. Search domains like <namespace>.svc.cluster.local, svc.cluster.local, cluster.local plus ndots can cause multiple lookup attempts. DNS request travels through Pod network to CoreDNS Service, then CoreDNS kubernetes plugin answers cluster records from API-watched Services/EndpointSlices. External domains use forwarding resolver configured in Corefile. NodeLocal DNSCache, if installed, alters the path and caching.

Pod app -> libc resolver -> /etc/resolv.conf -> DNS Service IP
        -> kube-proxy/eBPF -> CoreDNS Pod -> kubernetes plugin OR upstream DNS

kubectl -n kube-system get deployment coredns
kubectl -n kube-system get svc kube-dns -o wide
kubectl -n kube-system get configmap coredns -o yaml
kubectl -n kube-system logs deploy/coredns --tail=100
kubectl -n netlab get svc echo

Where: within a debug pod, not on the admin workstation, for cluster resolution:

cat /etc/resolv.conf
nslookup echo.netlab.svc.cluster.local
nslookup kubernetes.default.svc.cluster.local

For an EKS cluster, AWS VPC resolver, DHCP options, VPC DNS settings and network egress affect upstream DNS. CoreDNS CrashLoopBackOff, wrong Corefile, lack of endpoints, or blocked UDP/TCP 53 can cause failures. Large DNS responses can fall back to TCP; permitting only UDP 53 is insufficient in some environments.

Incident: only short names fail

FQDN works but echo fails: compare namespace, search domains, ndots, resolver behavior, and name collisions. echo in namespace frontend searches echo.frontend.svc...; service actually echo.netlab.svc... needs echo.netlab or FQDN.

Interview

Q: Is CoreDNS used when app calls a Pod IP directly? A: No DNS lookup is required. Q: Why can DNS work but application fail? A: DNS proves name resolution only; Service endpoints, routing, TLS and listener may still fail.

Chapter 08 — Ingress, AWS ALB, Nginx, TLS and end-to-end HTTP flow

Layer boundaries

Route 53 maps domain to LB DNS/address, not to a Pod. ALB receives HTTP(S) requests, evaluates listeners/rules, chooses target group. AWS Load Balancer Controller reconciles Ingress/Service objects to ALB AWS resources when configured. In instance target mode, ALB forwards to node NodePort and cluster dataplane forwards to Pod. In IP target mode, ALB registers Pod IPs directly (with compatible network setup). Nginx Ingress Controller, if deployed, is a separate proxy architecture; do not combine both flows as though they are identical.

Client -> Route53 DNS -> ALB HTTPS listener -> target group
    instance mode: NodeIP:NodePort -> Service -> Pod
    ip mode:       PodIP:containerPort (registered target)

apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: Ingress
metadata:
  name: echo
  namespace: netlab
  annotations:
    alb.ingress.kubernetes.io/scheme: internal
    alb.ingress.kubernetes.io/target-type: ip
spec:
  ingressClassName: alb
  rules:
  - host: echo.internal.example.com
    http:
      paths:
      - path: /
        pathType: Prefix
        backend:
          service:
            name: echo
            port: {number: 80}

This is illustrative, not deployable alone: requires AWS Load Balancer Controller, correctly tagged subnets, IAM workload identity, networking, and DNS/TLS configuration. For production add HTTPS listener, ACM certificate, security group design, access logs, WAF as appropriate, target health checks, and infrastructure ownership. Do not publicly expose the example echo service.

ALB 502 vs 503 vs 504 investigation

  • 502: ALB receives invalid response or connection problem to target; check target connection reset, TLS backend mismatch, application crashes, access logs.
  • 503: no registered/healthy targets or capacity/target selection issue; inspect target group and endpoints.
  • 504: target did not respond before timeout or connection attempt timed out; compare ALB idle timeout, app latency, SG/NACL and backend request trace.
kubectl -n netlab get ingress echo -o yaml
kubectl -n netlab get svc echo -o yaml
kubectl -n netlab get endpointslices -l kubernetes.io/service-name=echo
kubectl -n kube-system get pods | grep -i load-balancer

Correlate ALB access logs, CloudWatch metrics HTTPCode_ELB_5XX_Count, TargetResponseTime, target health reasons and app logs by request ID/time window. Use Route 53 dig to verify DNS separately from application path.

Interview

Q: Does an ALB Ingress require Nginx? A: No. AWS Load Balancer Controller can manage ALB directly. Q: Why does curl PodIP work but public URL return 504? A: Pod reachability bypasses LB/DNS/TLS/target group/SG layers; isolate each hop.

Chapter 09 — NetworkPolicy, RBAC, EKS identity and security boundaries

Policy mechanics

NetworkPolicy is an API declaration; enforcement requires CNI support. A Pod is non-isolated for a direction until selected by a policy governing that direction. Once isolated, allowed traffic is the union of applicable allow rules; standard NetworkPolicy does not define ordered deny rules. Both egress policy at source and ingress policy at destination must allow a flow if both sides are isolated. DNS egress must be explicitly permitted when applying default deny.

apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: NetworkPolicy
metadata:
  name: echo-only-from-client
  namespace: netlab
spec:
  podSelector:
    matchLabels: {app: echo}
  policyTypes: [Ingress]
  ingress:
  - from:
    - podSelector:
        matchLabels: {role: client}
    ports:
    - protocol: TCP
      port: 8080

This restricts inbound TCP/8080 to Pods labeled role=client in same namespace, only if CNI enforces NetworkPolicy. It does not restrict egress and does not enforce identity of an external ALB. Test policies in staging and maintain emergency recovery path. Be careful: Service DNAT order relative to policy evaluation is implementation-specific.

Identity separation

Kubernetes RBAC controls API actions, not packet flow. AWS IAM controls AWS API calls, not native Kubernetes Pod-to-Pod traffic. EKS Pod Identity or IRSA associates AWS credentials with workloads under distinct trust/configuration mechanisms; neither replaces Kubernetes NetworkPolicy. ServiceAccount token projection and audience matter. Avoid static AWS keys in Secrets and node-wide overprivileged IAM roles.

kubectl auth can-i get secrets --as=system:serviceaccount:netlab:default -n netlab
kubectl -n netlab get networkpolicies
kubectl -n netlab describe networkpolicy echo-only-from-client

Security incident

Workload unexpectedly accesses S3: inspect IAM role credential source, Pod Identity association/IRSA annotation, node role fallback, endpoint policies, CloudTrail and application SDK credential chain. Do not assume NetworkPolicy can stop AWS API calls by itself.

Interview

Q: Does RBAC prevent a compromised Pod from calling another Pod's port? A: No; RBAC governs Kubernetes API permissions. NetworkPolicy and app-layer authentication address network/application traffic.

Chapter 10 — Production troubleshooting playbook: layered diagnosis

First five minutes: protect service

  1. Establish incident severity, time, affected namespaces/services, blast radius, recent changes and SLO impact. Avoid destructive commands.
  2. Verify context and read-only cluster state: kubectl config current-context, get nodes, get pods -A, get events -A --sort-by=.lastTimestamp.
  3. Identify exact failing path: direct PodIP, Service ClusterIP, DNS, Ingress/ALB, public hostname. Test from a Pod in the same network policy context as the failing client.
  4. Collect evidence: request IDs, timestamps, target health, EndpointSlices, readiness, CNI logs, node routes, DNS queries, packet captures.
  5. If recent rollout caused outage, use approved rollback with impact analysis: kubectl rollout history deployment/<name> then kubectl rollout undo ... only after confirming the correct revision and downstream compatibility.

Symptom matrix

Symptom First evidence Common root cause Safe next action
kubectl timeout verbose client logs, DNS/TLS, API endpoint endpoint unreachable, SG, VPN, control-plane pressure network path verification
Pod Pending describe pod, node allocatable taints, insufficient requests, PVC AZ adjust constraint/capacity after evidence
Pod ContainerCreating events, kubelet, CNI/CSI image pull, IP allocation, mount isolate subsystem
Direct PodIP fails listener, route, policy, tcpdump app binds localhost, CNI route, policy compare source/destination captures
Service fails but PodIP works Service selector, EndpointSlices, targetPort no ready endpoints or wrong port fix selectors/probes/port
DNS name fails, IP works resolv.conf, CoreDNS, Corefile DNS service, policy, upstream inspect DNS path
Ingress fails, Service works ALB target health, SG, controller logs target registration, TLS, health check inspect ALB/controller
Intermittent timeout conntrack, MTU, app saturation flow exhaustion, PMTU, resource bottleneck time-correlated metrics

Reproducible diagnostic shell snippets

Admin workstation:

NS=netlab
kubectl -n "$NS" get pods -o wide
kubectl -n "$NS" get svc,ingress
kubectl -n "$NS" get endpointslices
kubectl -n "$NS" get events --sort-by=.lastTimestamp | tail -30
kubectl -n "$NS" logs deploy/echo --all-containers --tail=100
kubectl -n "$NS" rollout history deploy/echo

Node shell (privileged access required):

ip -s link
ip route
sudo ss -s
sudo journalctl -u kubelet --since '-15 min' --no-pager
sudo dmesg --ctime | tail -60

Never run iptables -F, ip route flush, delete CNI DaemonSets, restart all kubelets, or drain nodes without controlled change/rollback. Debugging should be read-only first.

Worked incident: service unreachable after Helm upgrade

Observed: curl http://echo.netlab.svc.cluster.local times out. Step A: check DNS nslookup; success means DNS path likely okay. Step B: kubectl get endpointslices: empty. Step C: kubectl get pods -l app=echo: no matching pods because chart changed label to app.kubernetes.io/name=echo. Step D: compare Service selector and Pod labels. Correct chart selector to match stable labels; helm template + helm diff (plugin, if installed) in staging; deploy; confirm EndpointSlices populated and successful request. Root cause: mismatched selector, not VPC outage. Prevention: contract tests checking selectors, endpoints and readiness.

Interview drill

Q: User says 'network issue'. What do you ask? A: source/destination/port/protocol, failure mode (DNS/timeout/reset/HTTP), time window, scope, recent changes, path and evidence. Q: How do you know issue is fixed? A: successful end-to-end requests, target health, latency/error SLO recovery, sustained monitoring and no regression.

Chapter 11 — Deep lab: inspect request path, inject safe failures, restore

Lab A: API and rollout

Where: admin workstation; disposable cluster only.

  1. Apply baseline manifest from chapter 1.
  2. kubectl -v=8 -n netlab get deployment echo and inspect request URL, HTTP status and timing.
  3. kubectl -n netlab get deploy,rs,pods -o wide; inspect ownerReferences to map Deployment→ReplicaSet→Pod.
  4. kubectl -n netlab scale deployment echo --replicas=3; watch kubectl -n netlab get pods -w.
  5. Verify kubectl -n netlab get endpointslices -l kubernetes.io/service-name=echo reflects ready Pods. Expected: replicas and endpoint count converge after readiness passes; count may temporarily differ while rollout settles.

Lab B: controlled selector break

Only disposable lab: patch Service selector to unmatched label:

kubectl -n netlab patch svc echo -p '{"spec":{"selector":{"app":"does-not-exist"}}}'
kubectl -n netlab get endpointslices -l kubernetes.io/service-name=echo
kubectl -n netlab patch svc echo -p '{"spec":{"selector":{"app":"echo"}}}'

Expected: ready endpoints disappear and return. Record observations. kubectl get svc still shows ClusterIP throughout. Restore immediately; do not do this in production.

Lab C: cross-node test plan

Schedule two distinct test Pods on different nodes using node affinity in a lab with at least two worker nodes; verify with kubectl get pods -o wide. Capture destination Pod IP and test TCP direct PodIP from source. On each node, capture tcpdump (authorized) and compare whether SYN leaves source, arrives destination and whether SYN-ACK returns. Interpret missing SYN vs missing SYN-ACK differently. Use CNI-specific tooling (cilium monitor, Calico policy logs, AWS VPC Flow Logs) where supported.

Lab D: NetworkPolicy

Deploy labeled client Pod and unlabeled client Pod; apply policy in chapter 9; test TCP connectivity. If both still connect, investigate whether CNI enforces NetworkPolicy and whether traffic is going through unexpected path; don't declare the policy effective merely because API accepted YAML. Remove policy after exercise.

Lab E: DNS and ingress

Test FQDN and short name from multiple namespaces. If AWS ALB controller exists in disposable EKS, deploy internal-only Ingress with ACM TLS and test health checks. Cost warning: EKS, ALB, NAT gateway and EC2 incur charges. Destroy via IaC after validation, review residual ENIs/LBs and billing.

Evidence sheet

For each lab record cluster version, CNI name/version, node OS/kernel, kube-proxy mode or replacement, source/destination IP, commands, sanitized outputs, expected vs actual, root cause, fix, rollback, and cost.

Chapter 12 — Senior interview question bank: model answers and follow-ups

Q1. kubectl get pods works but kubectl exec fails. Why? get reads API objects; exec requires streaming path through API server/kubelet and RBAC on pods/exec. Inspect exact error (403 vs transport), kubelet connectivity, node health and runtime. Follow-up: why can logs fail separately? pods/log is a different subresource and depends on kubelet/log availability.

Q2. Explain Deployment creation step-by-step. Client authenticates to API, object validated/stored, Deployment controller reconciles ReplicaSet, RS controller creates Pods, scheduler binds, kubelet creates sandbox/containers, CNI sets networking, probes mark readiness, EndpointSlices update, dataplane sends traffic. Follow-up: does etcd directly start a container? No.

Q3. Two Pods on same node communicate how? Pod namespace route -> virtual interface/CNI datapath -> destination interface -> destination socket, subject to policy. Follow-up: are all CNIs Linux bridges? No.

Q4. Two Pods across nodes communicate how? CNI provides routing/encapsulation/VPC-native path; underlay carries packet, destination node forwards to Pod. Follow-up: what changes in EKS VPC CNI? Pod IPs are VPC-routable in common EC2 mode.

Q5. What is kube-proxy? Service VIP traffic forwarding implementation, historically iptables/IPVS and newer alternatives depending on version; not a Pod scheduler or CNI specification. Follow-up: can Cilium replace it? In supported kube-proxy-replacement mode, yes.

Q6. What does CoreDNS do? Resolves Kubernetes Service/Pod DNS records and forwards external queries per configuration; does not allocate Pod IPs. Follow-up: what if CoreDNS healthy but DNS fails? Policy, resolver settings, Service forwarding or upstream can fail.

Q7. Why Service has no endpoints? Selector mismatch, no ready Pods, missing EndpointSlice updates or explicit selectorless Service. Follow-up: can a Running Pod be excluded? Yes if not Ready.

Q8. What is difference between Ingress and Service? Service abstracts endpoint routing at cluster networking layer; Ingress specifies HTTP(S) routing rules implemented by controller. Follow-up: is Ingress itself a proxy? No, controller provisions/configures proxy/LB.

Q9. How does ALB IP target mode differ from instance mode? IP mode registers Pod IPs; instance mode targets node NodePorts. Follow-up: why might IP mode fail? incompatible pod routing, controller, target group health or SG.

Q10. What happens on DNS lookup of api.backend? Pod resolver expands search suffixes, sends query to cluster DNS, CoreDNS resolves Service record, returns ClusterIP (or endpoints for headless). Follow-up: what if ndots causes excessive lookups? Latency and upstream load; tune cautiously.

Q11. Why is ping not sufficient to test service? ICMP may be blocked or take different path; TCP listener, TLS, app readiness and policy still matter. Follow-up: which test? curl/openssl/ss and packet capture at relevant hop.

Q12. Why does Pod IP change? Pod recreation can allocate new IP; use Service DNS and avoid hardcoding. Follow-up: how do StatefulSets help? Stable DNS identity via headless Service, not guaranteed immutable IP.

Q13. What is conntrack? Kernel connection tracking used by NAT/stateful packet handling; exhaustion can cause new flow failures. Follow-up: how detect? kernel logs, conntrack counters and flow metrics.

Q14. What is NetworkPolicy default deny? Select Pods with policyTypes ingress/egress and no allow rules for that direction; enforcement requires compatible CNI. Follow-up: why allow DNS egress? Otherwise name resolution may fail.

Q15. Why do small requests work but large ones hang? MTU/PMTUD blackhole or application limits; trace path and packet size, don't assume DNS. Follow-up: VXLAN overhead effect? Reduced effective MTU.

Q16. How does EKS Pod Identity differ from NetworkPolicy? Pod Identity supplies AWS IAM credentials; NetworkPolicy controls network reachability. Follow-up: how to prevent credential abuse? least privilege, audit, identity association, egress controls.

Q17. What happens when etcd quorum lost? Writes cannot be committed without quorum; control plane degrades. Running workloads can continue but management/reconciliation suffers. Follow-up: restore? Follow vendor-supported snapshot/restore procedure with validated backups and quorum plan.

Q18. What is scheduler's role in CNI? Scheduler assigns node; kubelet/runtime/CNI prepare networking afterward. Follow-up: does scheduler choose Pod IP? Usually no.

Q19. Difference between readiness and liveness? Readiness gates Service endpoints; liveness can restart container; startup probe protects slow initialization. Follow-up: why avoid aggressive liveness? Restart loops worsen overload.

Q20. What is your 504 incident method? Determine whether ALB or upstream generated it, check target health, SG/NACL, app latency, timeouts, logs, EndpointSlices and correlate timestamps. Follow-up: what evidence closes incident? sustained successful requests and recovered SLO.

Mock interview scoring

For each answer score 0–2 each: conceptual accuracy, internal sequence, production evidence/commands, security/rollback, and clarity. Total 10. Under 7: redo corresponding lab and explain packet/control flow without notes. Above 8 consistently across scenarios indicates stronger readiness but does not guarantee selection.

Glossary

  • API Server: Kubernetes API gateway and validation/storage coordination point.
  • CNI: Interface/specification for container networking integrations.
  • CRI: Container Runtime Interface used by kubelet.
  • CSI: Container Storage Interface.
  • EndpointSlice: Scalable representation of Service backends.
  • ENI: Elastic Network Interface in AWS.
  • IRSA: IAM Roles for Service Accounts (EKS).
  • NAT/DNAT: Network Address Translation / destination translation.
  • PMTUD: Path MTU Discovery.
  • RTO/RPO: Recovery time / recovery point objectives.
  • Veth: Virtual Ethernet pair.
